# The library

Generated by `tools/apidoc.py` from the library's sources (`lib/`). Everything here is there in every program, without imports. Functions can be called as `f(x, y)` or `x.f(y)`. Each topic starts with a whole program to try.

**For a first game:**

- Shapes: `clear(color)`, `circle(center, radius, color)`, `rect(x, y, w, h, color)`, `line(a, b, width, color)`; colors are `rgb(r, g, b)` from 0 to 1
- Text: `text(s, pos, size, color)`, `text_centered(s, center, size)`; `shadow = rgba(0, 0, 0, 0.7)` to read over a busy scene
- Pictures: `hero = load_texture(embed("hero.png"))` once, then `sprite(hero, pos)` in draw
- Input: `input_axis()` (arrows, WASD, a stick), `key_pressed(.space)` (this frame), `key_down(.left)` (held), `mouse_pos()`, `mouse_pressed(.left)`
- Random: `random()` (0 to 1), `random(lo, hi)`, `random_int(lo, hi)` (hi left out), `random_item(xs)`
- Sound: `s = sfx_coin()` (also `sfx_jump`, `sfx_hit`, `sfx_chime`...) once, then `play(s)`; `melody("C4 E4 G4", 120.0)` for music
- Menus: `ui_begin("title", pos)`, then `if ui_button("Play"): ...`, then `ui_end()`; a game's own buttons anywhere: `if hud_button(Rect(20, 620, 160, 44), "Build"): ...`, and `hud_hovered()` so the click is not an order too
- Timers: `every(1, 2.0)` is true once every 2 seconds (the 1 names the timer: another timer is `every(2, 0.5)`); or count down a number yourself: `wait -= dt`
- Collisions: `circles_overlap(a, ra, b, rb)`, `overlaps(rect_a, rect_b)`, `Rect(x, y, w, h)`
- Camera: `camera2d(player_pos)` at the top of draw keeps the player in the middle; `screen_to_world(mouse_pos())` is where the mouse points in the world (in update too); `reset_transform()` before drawing the score
- Effects: `sparks: Particles`, then `sparks.emit(pos, 20, 200.0, YELLOW, life = 0.5, size = 3.0)`, `sparks.update(dt)` and `sparks.draw()`; `shake: Shake`, `shake.add(0.5)`, `shake.update(dt)`, `camera2d(target + shake.offset())`
- Keeping data: `save_data("best", "{score}")`, `load_data("best") ?? ""`

## Built-in functions

Part of the language itself: printing, stopping with an error, and a few functions the compiler knows.

```gdscript
# example: built-in functions
print("hello", 42, 3.5, [1, 2, 3])      # any values, separated by spaces
xs = fill(0, 5)                          # five zeros
xs[2] = 7
assert(xs.len() == 5, "five of them")   # stops the program if not true
print(xs, sqrt(2.0), size_of(int))
b = bytes("hi")                          # a string's bytes, as u8[]
print(b, str_from_bytes(b))
if xs[2] != 7: panic("xs[2] changed")   # stop, with a message and where it happened
```

- `print(values...)`: prints the values, separated by spaces, and a line break
- `assert(ok: bool, message: str = "")`: stops the program (with the message, and where) if `ok` is false; with no message, a comparison says what its sides were: `assertion failed: total == 10 (total is 9)`
- `panic(message: str)`: stops the program, with the message and where each function on the way was called
- `unwrap(r) -> T`: the value of an optional, or of a result that can be an error (`int | ParseError`); none or an error stops the program, saying which and where
- `expect(r, message: str) -> T`: the same, saying the message first when it stops: `config = read_file(path).expect("the settings file")`
- `fill[T](x: T, n: int) -> T[]`: an array of `n` copies of `x`
- `sqrt(x: f64) -> f64`: the square root
- `sqrt(x: f32) -> f32`
- `embed(path: str) -> u8[]`: a file's contents, read when the program is compiled and kept in it (the path is relative to the source file)
- `bytes(s: str) -> u8[]`: a string's bytes (UTF-8)
- `str_from_bytes(b: u8[]) -> str`: a string of these bytes
- `size_of(T) -> int`: how many bytes a value of type T takes, and its alignment
- `align_of(T) -> int`
- `int(x)`: a number as a whole number of that type (a float is cut toward zero: int(3.7) is 3); text is read with `to_int(s)`, and `c'A'` is a character's code (65)
- `i32(x)`
- `u8(x)`
- `f64(x)`: a number as a float of that size (f32 and f64 also mix by themselves)
- `f32(x)`
- `str(x)`: any value as text, as `print` shows it

## Arrays

`T[]` is a growable array. Arrays are values: assigning one or passing it to a function gives a copy (shared until one of them changes). Functions can be called as `f(xs, y)` or `xs.f(y)`. Binary search, orderings and choices (`lower_bound`, `next_permutation`, `combinations`): algo.jo below.

```gdscript
# example: arrays
scores = [12, 40, 7, 33]
scores.push(25)                          # add at the end
scores.sort()
print(scores)                            # [7, 12, 25, 33, 40]
print(scores.len(), scores.first(), scores.last(), scores.sum())

big = scores.filter((s): s > 20)         # a new array: [25, 33, 40]
print(big.map((s): s * 2))
print(scores.any((s): s > 35), scores.count((s): s % 2 == 1))

loop i in 0.. and s in scores:           # an index and the elements together
    print("{i}: {s}")

top = scores.pop()                       # take the last one off
scores.insert(0, 1)                      # put one in at the front
print(top, scores, scores.contains(12), scores.index_of(25))

print(scores.lower_bound(25), scores.join(" "))   # (scores is sorted) where 25 goes; as text
```

#### Built in

- `len(xs: T[]) -> int`: how many elements
- `push(xs: mut T[], x: T)`: adds `x` at the end
- `pop(xs: mut T[]) -> T`: removes the last element and gives it back
- `insert(xs: mut T[], i: int, x: T)`: puts `x` in at index `i`, moving the ones after it along
- `remove_at(xs: mut T[], i: int) -> T`: takes out the element at `i` and gives it back (`remove(x)` takes out a value)
- `extend(xs: mut T[], ys: T[])`: adds all of `ys` at the end
- `clear(xs: mut T[])`: removes every element
- `truncate(xs: mut T[], n: int)`: keeps the first `n` elements
- `resize(xs: mut T[], n: int)`: makes it `n` long: new elements are zero
- `reserve(xs: mut T[], n: int)`: makes room for `n` elements without growing again; `cap` is the room there is
- `cap(xs: T[]) -> int`

### array.jo

Generic array utilities. Arrays are values with copy-on-write storage.

- `is_empty[T] = (xs: T[]) -> bool`
- `first[T] = (xs: T[]) -> T`
- `last[T] = (xs: T[]) -> T`
- `first[T] = (xs: T[], n: int) -> T[]`: the first n elements (all of them when there are fewer: xs[..n] would panic)
- `last[T] = (xs: T[], n: int) -> T[]`: the last n elements (all of them when there are fewer)
- `get[T] = (xs: T[], i: int) -> T?`: element i, or none past either end (xs[i] stops the program there): xs.get(i) ?? fallback
- `contains[T] = (xs: T[], x: T) -> bool`
- `index_of[T] = (xs: T[], x: T) -> int`
- `map[T, U] = (xs: T[], f: (T) -> U) -> U[]`
- `filter[T] = (xs: T[], f: (T) -> bool) -> T[]`
- `retain[T] = (xs: mut T[], keep: (T) -> bool)`: keep only the elements for which keep is true, in their order (in place: bullets.retain((b): b.alive))
- `remove[T] = (xs: mut T[], x: T) -> bool`: take out the first element equal to x (Python's list.remove); false when there is none. By position: xs.remove_at(i)
- `each[T] = (xs: T[], f: (T) -> void)`
- `reduce[T, A] = (xs: T[], init: A, f: (A, T) -> A) -> A`
- `find_index[T] = (xs: T[], f: (T) -> bool) -> int`: the index of the first element f is true for, or -1
- `any[T] = (xs: T[], f: (T) -> bool) -> bool`
- `all[T] = (xs: T[], f: (T) -> bool) -> bool`
- `find[T] = (xs: T[], f: (T) -> bool) -> T?`
- `count[T] = (xs: T[], f: (T) -> bool) -> int`
- `sum[T] = (xs: T[]) -> T`
- `min[T] = (xs: T[]) -> T`
- `max[T] = (xs: T[]) -> T`
- `min[T] = (xs: T[], default: T) -> T`: (the smallest or largest, or `default` when xs is empty: `xs.max(default = 0)`)
- `max[T] = (xs: T[], default: T) -> T`
- `min[T] = (a: T, b: T) -> T`: the smaller and the larger of two values of a type with its own `<` (a big number, a version)
- `max[T] = (a: T, b: T) -> T`
- `min_by[T, K] = (xs: T[], key: (T) -> K) -> T`: the element with the smallest key, or the largest (the first of equal ones): the cheapest item, the nearest enemy (min_by(enemies, (e): distance(e.pos, me)))
- `max_by[T, K] = (xs: T[], key: (T) -> K) -> T`
- `min[T, K] = (xs: T[], key: (T) -> K) -> T`: the element with the smallest key, as Python's `min(words, key = len)` (min_by)
- `max[T, K] = (xs: T[], key: (T) -> K) -> T`: the element with the largest key, as Python's `max(words, key = len)` (max_by)
- `unique[T] = (xs: T[]) -> T[]`: each different element once, in the order they first come
- `group_by[T, K] = (xs: T[], key: (T) -> K) -> {K: T[]}`: the elements by a key: each key with the elements that have it, in order (keys in the order they first come)
- `reverse[T] = (xs: mut T[])`
- `reversed[T] = (xs: T[]) -> T[]`
- `range = (a: int, b: int) -> int[]`
- `enumerate[T] = (xs: T[]) -> (int, T)[]`
- `zip[A, B] = (xs: A[], ys: B[]) -> (A, B)[]`
- `flatten[T] = (xss: T[][]) -> T[]`
- `swap[T] = (xs: mut T[], i: int, j: int)`: the elements at i and j trade places

#### Sorting

- `(<)[A, B] = (a: (A, B), b: (A, B)) -> bool`: tuples compare element by element, the first that differs deciding (sorting (score, name) pairs sorts by score, then name)
- `(<)[A, B, C] = (a: (A, B, C), b: (A, B, C)) -> bool`
- `sort[T] = (xs: mut T[], descending: bool = false)`: sort in place, smallest first (using <), or largest first; equal elements keep their order
- `sort_with[T] = (xs: mut T[], less: (T, T) -> bool)`: sort in place with a custom "less than" function (equal elements keep their order)
- `sort_by[T, K] = (xs: mut T[], key: (T) -> K, descending: bool = false)`: sort in place by a key, smallest first or largest first (equal keys keep their order: sort by name, then by score, for the scores in order of name)
- `sorted[T] = (xs: T[], descending: bool = false) -> T[]`: a sorted copy of xs
- `sorted_by[T, K] = (xs: T[], key: (T) -> K, descending: bool = false) -> T[]`: a copy of xs sorted by a key
- `sorted[T, K] = (xs: T[], key: (T) -> K, descending: bool = false) -> T[]`: a copy of xs sorted by a key, as Python's `sorted(words, key = len)` (sorted_by)
- `sort[T, K] = (xs: mut T[], key: (T) -> K, descending: bool = false)`: sort in place by a key, as Python's `xs.sort(key = (p): p.age)` (sort_by)

### algo.jo

Searching sorted arrays, orderings and choices, as C++'s <algorithm> and Python's bisect and itertools have them:

```gdscript
xs = [1 3 3 5 8]
lower_bound(xs, 3)           # 1: the first index whose element is not less than 3
upper_bound(xs, 3)           # 3: the first index whose element is more than 3
partition_point(xs, (x): x < 4)   # 3: the first index where the condition stops holding
loop next_permutation(p):    # each ordering of p after the one it has, in sorted order
combinations(xs, 2)          # every choice of 2, as arrays
```

The binary searches take time in proportion to the log of the length; the array must be sorted (as by sort()) for their answers to mean anything.

- `lower_bound[T] = (xs: T[], x: T) -> int`: the first index i where xs[i] is not less than x (xs.len() when there is none): where x would go in the sorted array before the ones equal to it (C++'s lower_bound; Python's bisect_left)
- `upper_bound[T] = (xs: T[], x: T) -> int`: the first index i where x is less than xs[i] (xs.len() when there is none): where x would go after the ones equal to it; upper_bound - lower_bound is how many equal x (C++'s upper_bound; Python's bisect_right)
- `bisect_left[T] = (xs: T[], x: T) -> int`: Python's name for lower_bound
- `bisect_right[T] = (xs: T[], x: T) -> int`: Python's name for upper_bound
- `partition_point[T] = (xs: T[], f: (T) -> bool) -> int`: the first index where f is false, for an f that is true for the first elements and false for the rest (xs.len() when it is true for all): partition_point(xs, (p): p.age < 30) in people sorted by age (Rust's partition_point; C++'s with a condition)
- `reverse[T] = (xs: mut T[], start: int, end: int)`: the elements from index start up to (not including) end reversed in place: xs[start..end] backwards (reverse(xs) turns the whole array)
- `next_permutation[T] = (xs: mut T[]) -> bool`: the next ordering of xs in sorted order (by <), in place, and true; for the last one (largest first) xs goes back to the first (sorted) and it gives false. Starting from a sorted array, `loop next_permutation(p):` after a first look at p goes through each ordering once, equal elements not repeating any (C++'s next_permutation)
- `prev_permutation[T] = (xs: mut T[]) -> bool`: the previous ordering of xs in sorted order, in place, and true; for the first one (sorted) xs goes to the last (largest first) and it gives false (C++'s prev_permutation)
- `permutations[T] = (xs: T[]) -> T[][]`: every ordering of xs, each an array: n! of them (10 elements are 3.6 million arrays; to go through them without keeping them all, sort and loop on next_permutation). In the order of Python's itertools.permutations: by the positions taken, so equal elements give repeats
- `permutations[T] = (xs: T[], k: int) -> T[][]`: every ordering of k of the elements of xs: n! / (n - k)! arrays of k (permutations([1 2 3], 2) is [[1, 2], [1, 3], [2, 1], [2, 3], [3, 1], [3, 2]])
- `combinations[T] = (xs: T[], k: int) -> T[][]`: every choice of k of the elements of xs, each an array in the order they are in xs: n! / (k! (n - k)!) of them (combinations([1 2 3 4], 2) is [[1, 2], [1, 3], [1, 4], [2, 3], [2, 4], [3, 4]], as Python's itertools.combinations; 30 choose 15 is 155 million arrays: count first)
- `join[T] = (xs: T[], sep: str = "") -> str`: the elements as text with sep between them, as print shows each: [1 2 3].join(" ") is "1 2 3" (an array of str joins as it is)

## Strings

`str` is text, in UTF-8. `"{x}"` puts a value in a string; `"{x:.2}"` rounds it to two decimals (more ways: [the language](LANGUAGE.md#2-lexical-structure)). Strings are values, like arrays.

```gdscript
# example: strings
name = "  Sloppy  ".trim()
print(name.upper(), name.len(), name.starts_with("Slo"))

words = "the quick brown fox".split(" ")
print(words, words.join("-"))

# {...} puts a value in a string; after a colon, how to show it
pi = 3.14159
print("pi is about {pi:.2}, a million is {1000000:,}, a quarter is {0.25:.0%}")
print("[{name:<10}] [{name:>10}] [{7:03}]")

n = to_int("42") ?? 0                    # none if it is not a number: 0 instead
print(n + 1, "ab" * 3, "a,b,c".replace(",", "; "))
print("héllo".len(), "héllo".char_count())   # bytes (UTF-8), and characters
```

String utilities. Strings are immutable UTF-8 byte sequences; indexing gives bytes.

- `is_digit = (c: u8) -> bool`
- `is_alpha = (c: u8) -> bool`
- `is_alnum = (c: u8) -> bool`
- `is_space = (c: u8) -> bool`
- `is_upper = (c: u8) -> bool`
- `is_lower = (c: u8) -> bool`
- `chr = (c: int) -> str`: the character with code point c, as text (UTF-8: one to four bytes); chr(233) is "é"
- `byte_str = (b: int) -> str`: a string of the one byte b (0 to 255; text in UTF-8 is made of these)
- `find = (s: str, sub: str, start: int = 0) -> int`
- `rfind = (s: str, sub: str) -> int`
- `find_byte = (s: str, c: u8, start: int = 0) -> int`
- `contains = (s: str, sub: str) -> bool`: whether a character (a byte: s[i]) is one of the text's: `c in "aeiou"`
- `contains = (s: str, c: u8) -> bool`
- `starts_with = (s: str, prefix: str) -> bool`
- `ends_with = (s: str, suffix: str) -> bool`
- `trim = (s: str) -> str`: remove spaces, tabs and line breaks from both ends
- `trim = (s: str, chars: str) -> str`: remove any of the characters in `chars` from both ends
- `trim_start = (s: str) -> str`
- `trim_end = (s: str) -> str`
- `split = (s: str, sep: str) -> str[]`: the parts of s between the separators (split("a,b", ",") is ["a", "b"]; with "", the characters). For a text's lines, `lines(s)`: split(s, "\n") keeps the "\r" of Windows line ends ("\r\n")
- `split = (s: str, sep: str, max: int) -> str[]`: split at the first `max` separators only: the rest stays in the last part (split("k=v=w", "=", 1) is ["k", "v=w"])
- `split = (s: str) -> str[]`: split on runs of whitespace (the same as words(s))
- `count = (s: str, sub: str) -> int`: how many times sub is in s, not overlapping ("banana".count("an") is 2)
- `pad_start = (s: str, width: int, fill: str = " ") -> str`: s made `width` characters long with `fill` before it (pad_start("7", 3, "0") is "007"), or after it; a longer s stays as it is ("{s:>10}" and "{s:<10}" in a string do the same with spaces)
- `pad_end = (s: str, width: int, fill: str = " ") -> str`
- `capitalize = (s: str) -> str`: the first letter in upper case ("hello world" -> "Hello world")
- `words = (s: str) -> str[]`: split on runs of whitespace
- `lines = (s: str) -> str[]`: the lines of s, without their line ends ("\n", or Windows' "\r\n")
- `join = (parts: str[], sep: str = "") -> str`: sep.join(parts), as in Python
- `join = (sep: str, parts: str[]) -> str`
- `replace = (s: str, old: str, new: str) -> str`
- `repeat = (s: str, n: int) -> str`
- `(*) = (s: str, n: int) -> str`
- `(*) = (n: int, s: str) -> str`
- `upper = (s: str) -> str`: s in capitals (letters of Latin, accented ones too, Greek and Cyrillic: "café" is "CAFÉ")
- `lower = (s: str) -> str`: s in small letters (as upper)
- `to_int = (s: str) -> int?`: parse an integer (decimal, or hex after 0x, binary after 0b, octal after 0o; _ may separate digits); none if it is not one or does not fit in an int. Spaces and line ends around it are left out (" 42\n" is 42, a line as read from a file); inside it they are not ("4 2" is none)
- `to_int = (s: str, base: int) -> int?`: an integer written in base 2 to 36 (to_int("ff", 16) is 255; letters for the digits past 9, in either case; the base's own prefix may come first: "0xff", "0b101")
- `ints = (text: str) -> int[]`: every whole number in the text, in order: each run of digits, negative when a `-` is right before it (ints("x=3,y=-4") is [3, -4], ints("1-3 a") is [1, -3]); in one pass, without splitting. A number too big for an int stops the program
- `to_float = (s: str) -> f64?`: Parse a decimal number. The result is the double nearest to the exact decimal value (ties to even), like C's strtod: an estimate, then exact big-integer comparisons. Spaces and line ends around it are left out, as for to_int.

#### Characters

- `reversed = (s: str) -> str`: the characters (code points) in reverse order (also as `reversed(s)`, as for an array)
- `reverse = (s: str) -> str`
- `without_last_char = (s: str) -> str`: s without its last character (all its bytes: backspace in a text field)
- `char_count = (s: str) -> int`: number of unicode code points
- `first = (s: str, n: int) -> str`: the first n characters (all of it when there are fewer; s[..n] counts bytes, and can cut an é in two)
- `last = (s: str, n: int) -> str`: the last n characters (all of it when there are fewer)
- `chars = (s: str) -> int[]`: unicode code points of s
- `hash = (s: str) -> u64`: a number made from the text: the same for the same text (for maps, and to pick by name)

## Regular expressions

`regex(r"...")` makes a pattern once; `s.find(r)`, `s.find_all(r)`, `s.matches(r)`, `s.replace(r, "$1")` and `s.split(r)` use it. Raw strings (`r"\d+"`) keep the backslashes as they are.

```gdscript
# example: regex
log = "2026-10-14 08:15 error: disk full\n2026-10-14 08:16 info: retrying\n2026-10-14 08:20 error: gave up"
line = regex(r"^(\S+) (\S+) (\w+): (.*)$")           # made once, used many times
loop m in log.find_all(line):
    if m.groups[2] == "error": print("{m.groups[1]} {m.groups[3]}")

print("ada@example.com".matches(regex(r"[\w.]+@[\w.]+\.\w+")))   # all of it matches
print("a1b22c333".find_all(regex(r"\d+")).map((m): m.text))
print("x = 1, y = 2".replace(regex(r"(\w) = (\d)"), "$2=$1"))
print("one, two;three".split(regex(r"[,;]\s*")))
print("Hello World".replace(regex(r"(?i)o"), "0"))
```

Regular expressions: patterns to find in text, test it against, take apart and replace in.

```gdscript
date = regex(r"(\d{4})-(\d\d)-(\d\d)")
if let m = "due 2026-10-14!".find(date): print(m.text, m.groups[0])   # 2026-10-14 2026
"2026-10-14".matches(date)                       # true: all of the text matches
"a1b22c333".find_all(regex(r"\d+")).map((m): m.text)   # ["1", "22", "333"]
"x = 1, y = 2".replace(regex(r"(\w) = (\d)"), "$2=$1")  # "1=x, 2=y"
"a, b;c".split(regex(r"[,;]\s*"))                       # ["a", "b", "c"]
```

Patterns: . (any character but a line break) [abc] [a-z] [^0-9] \d \w \s (and \D \W \S) ^ $ \b \B, x* x+ x? x{2} x{2,} x{2,5} (and lazy x*? x+? x?? x{2,5}?), a|b, (group) (?:group), \ before any of .[]{}()*+?^$|\ for itself, \n \t \r; (?i) at the start ignores case (in ASCII). \w takes letters of any script ("Zürich" is one word). A pattern a user typed is checked first with regex_error(pattern) (regex(pattern) stops the program when it is wrong). Matching takes time in proportion to the text's length times the pattern's (it never backtracks without end), and works on characters (code points), not bytes. Make a Regex once and use it for every text: regex() reads and prepares the pattern each time. On a long text, a pattern without ^ $ \b \B is matched a character at a time by a table made as it goes (a DFA), a few nanoseconds a character.

- `struct Regex`
  ```gdscript
  struct Regex:
      pattern: str
      prog: __ReIns[]
      classes: __ReClass[]
      groups: int             # how many ( ) groups it has
      icase = false
      code: int[]
      amap: int[]
      crange: int[]
      cstart: int[]
      cneg: int[]
      smap: int[]
      start_wide = true
      start_any = true
      prefix = ""
      dfa = false
      rcode: int[]
  ```
- `struct Match`: one match: where it is (byte offsets), its text, and the text of each ( ) group ("" for a group that took no part)
  ```gdscript
  struct Match:
      start: int
      end: int
      text: str
      groups: str[]
  ```

#### Using one

- `regex_error = (pattern: str) -> str?`: what is wrong with a pattern, or none when regex() can read it: for a pattern a user typed, asked before regex(), which stops the program at one it cannot read
  ```gdscript
  if let why = regex_error(pat): fail("bad pattern: {why}")
  ```
- `regex = (pattern: str) -> Regex`: a pattern made ready to match (a pattern it cannot read panics, saying where: regex_error asks first)
- `find = (s: str, r: Regex, from: int = 0) -> Match?`: the first match in s (from byte offset `from` on), or none
- `find_all = (s: str, r: Regex) -> Match[]`: every match in s, from left to right, not overlapping
- `matches = (s: str, r: Regex) -> bool`: the whole of s matches r (not only a part of it)
- `contains = (s: str, r: Regex) -> bool`: r matches somewhere in s
- `replace = (s: str, r: Regex, with: str) -> str`: s with each match of r replaced by `with`, where $0 is the match and $1, $2 ... its groups ($$ for a $)
- `replace = (s: str, r: Regex, f: (Match) -> str) -> str`: s with each match of r replaced by what f makes of it
- `split = (s: str, r: Regex) -> str[]`: the parts of s between the matches of r

## Maps

`{K: V}` maps keys to values, in the order they were added.

```gdscript
# example: maps
ages = {"ada": 36, "alan": 41}
ages["grace"] = 85                       # add or change
print(ages["ada"], ages.len(), ages.has("bob"))
print(ages.get("bob") ?? 0)              # none for a missing key: 0 instead

counts: {str: int} = {}
loop w in "a rose is a rose is a rose".split(" "):
    counts[w] += 1                       # a missing key starts at 0
loop word, n in counts:                  # in the order they were added
    print("{word}: {n}")
counts.remove("is")
print(counts.keys(), counts.values())
```

Hash map {K: V}: insertion ordered, open addressing over a dense entry list. The first two fields must stay `keys` and `vals` (the compiler iterates them in for loops, of a map with nothing gone: __map_live, __map_tidy). A removed entry stays in place, marked gone (so the others keep theirs), its slot a tombstone (1 << 32: probes go on past it); when half of them are gone, they are taken out at once.

- `struct Map[K, V]`
  ```gdscript
  struct Map[K, V]:
      keys: K[]
      vals: V[]
      slots: u64[]      # 0 = empty, otherwise the entry's hash's upper 32 bits | entry index + 1
      hashes: u64[]
      gone: bool[]      # (entries removed since the last compaction: true; shorter than keys when
                        # entries were added after the last removal)
      ngone: int
  ```
- `hash = (x: int) -> u64`: full 64-bit avalanche (murmur3 finalizer): every key bit affects the low bits used for slots, so keys like multiples of 4096 or float bit patterns spread out
- `hash = (x: u64) -> u64`
- `hash = (x: bool) -> u64`
- `hash = (x)`
- `([]=)[K, V] = (m: mut Map[K, V], k: K, v: V)`
- `reserve[K, V] = (m: mut Map[K, V], n: int)`: room for n entries without growing (when many are to come)
- `([])[K, V] = (m: Map[K, V], k: K) -> V`
- `get[K, V] = (m: Map[K, V], k: K) -> V?`
- `get[K, V] = (m: Map[K, V], k: K, default: V) -> V`
- `contains[K, V] = (m: Map[K, V], k: K) -> bool`
- `has[K, V] = (m: Map[K, V], k: K) -> bool`
- `len[K, V] = (m: Map[K, V]) -> int`
- `is_empty[K, V] = (m: Map[K, V]) -> bool`
- `keys[K, V] = (m: Map[K, V]) -> K[]`
- `values[K, V] = (m: Map[K, V]) -> V[]`
- `items[K, V] = (m: Map[K, V]) -> (K, V)[]`: (the (key, value) pairs, in the order added: for sorting them, `m.items().sort_by(...)`)
- `clear[K, V] = (m: mut Map[K, V])`
- `remove[K, V] = (m: mut Map[K, V], k: K) -> bool`: remove a key; returns true if it was present. The others keep their order.
- `(==)[K, V] = (a: Map[K, V], b: Map[K, V]) -> bool`: the same keys with equal values (in any order) (not for sloppy0: it would take the generic (==) for all structs)
- `hash[K, V] = (m: Map[K, V]) -> u64`: a number made from the entries, in any order: maps that are == hash alike (so a map can be a key). (Each entry's key and value hashed together, the entries' added up)

## Math and random numbers

For vectors and matrices, see [colors, vectors and matrices](#colors-vectors-and-matrices). `int` is 64 bits: `gcd`, `isqrt`, `pow_mod` and the others in integer.jo are exact for all of them. `+`, `-` and `*` on integers wrap around; `checked_add` and the others below say when they would. Past 64 bits: [big integers](#big-integers), exact, and [big numbers](#big-numbers), a float with a large exponent (about 15 digits).

```gdscript
# example: math and random numbers
print(PI, sqrt(2.0), abs(-3), floor(2.7), round(2.5))
print(min(3, 8), max(2.5, 1.0), clamp(15, 0, 10))
print(sin(PI / 2.0), atan2(1.0, 1.0), degrees(PI), pow(2.0, 10.0))
print(lerp(0.0, 100.0, 0.25), smoothstep(0.0, 1.0, 0.5), mod(-1, 3))
print(gcd(12, 18), lcm(4, 6), isqrt(10 ^ 18), pow_mod(3, 10 ^ 18, 1_000_000_007), inv_mod(3, 7))
print(checked_add(MAX_INT, 1), saturating_add(u8(250), 10), checked_cast[u8](300), wrapping_mul(u8(16), 16))

seed_random(7)                           # the same numbers every run
print(random(), random(10.0, 20.0), random_int(1, 7))

r = rng(123)                             # a generator of its own
print(r.next_int(0, 100), r.next_float())
```

### math.jo

Math basics.

- `const PI = 3.141592653589793`
- `const TAU = 6.283185307179586`
- `const E = 2.718281828459045`
- `const INF = 1e308 * 10.0`
- `const NAN = INF - INF`
- `const MAX_INT = 9223372036854775807`
- `const MIN_INT = -9223372036854775807 - 1`
- `abs = (x: int) -> int`
- `abs = (x: f64) -> f64`
- `abs = (x: f32) -> f32`
- `min = (a: int, b: int) -> int`
- `max = (a: int, b: int) -> int`
- `min = (a: f64, b: f64) -> f64`
- `max = (a: f64, b: f64) -> f64`
- `min = (a: f32, b: f32) -> f32`
- `max = (a: f32, b: f32) -> f32`
- `min = (a: int, b: int, c: int) -> int`: (of three: max(a, b, c))
- `max = (a: int, b: int, c: int) -> int`
- `min = (a: f64, b: f64, c: f64) -> f64`
- `max = (a: f64, b: f64, c: f64) -> f64`
- `clamp = (x: int, lo: int, hi: int) -> int`
- `clamp = (x: f64, lo: f64, hi: f64) -> f64`
- `clamp = (x: f32, lo: f32, hi: f32) -> f32`
- `abs = (x: i32) -> i32`: the other integer types keep their type (the int ones above take mixed ones: min(i8, u8))
- `abs = (x: i16) -> i16`
- `abs = (x: i8) -> i8`
- `min = (a: i32, b: i32) -> i32`
- `min = (a: u32, b: u32) -> u32`
- `min = (a: i16, b: i16) -> i16`
- `min = (a: u16, b: u16) -> u16`
- `min = (a: i8, b: i8) -> i8`
- `min = (a: u8, b: u8) -> u8`
- `min = (a: u64, b: u64) -> u64`
- `max = (a: i32, b: i32) -> i32`
- `max = (a: u32, b: u32) -> u32`
- `max = (a: i16, b: i16) -> i16`
- `max = (a: u16, b: u16) -> u16`
- `max = (a: i8, b: i8) -> i8`
- `max = (a: u8, b: u8) -> u8`
- `max = (a: u64, b: u64) -> u64`
- `clamp = (x: i32, lo: i32, hi: i32) -> i32`
- `clamp = (x: u32, lo: u32, hi: u32) -> u32`
- `clamp = (x: i16, lo: i16, hi: i16) -> i16`
- `clamp = (x: u16, lo: u16, hi: u16) -> u16`
- `clamp = (x: i8, lo: i8, hi: i8) -> i8`
- `clamp = (x: u8, lo: u8, hi: u8) -> u8`
- `clamp = (x: u64, lo: u64, hi: u64) -> u64`
- `sign = (x: f64) -> f64`
- `sign = (x: int) -> int`
- `floor = (x: f64) -> f64`
- `ceil = (x: f64) -> f64`
- `round = (x: f64) -> f64`: the nearest whole number; halves go away from zero (round(2.5) is 3.0, round(-2.5) is -3.0; Python's round goes to the even one, 2)
- `trunc = (x: f64) -> f64`
- `fract = (x: f64) -> f64`
- `mod = (x: f64, y: f64) -> f64`
- `mod = (x: int, y: int) -> int`
- `is_nan = (x: f64) -> bool`
- `is_finite = (x: f64) -> bool`: an ordinary number: not infinite and not NaN
- `is_inf = (x: f64) -> bool`
- `lerp = (a: f64, b: f64, t: f64) -> f64`
- `mix = (a: f64, b: f64, t: f64) -> f64`
- `inverse_lerp = (a: f64, b: f64, x: f64) -> f64`: where x is between a and b, from 0 (at a) to 1 (at b): lerp backwards
- `remap = (x: f64, a: f64, b: f64, c: f64, d: f64) -> f64`: x moved from the range a..b to c..d, as p5's map: remap(mouse.x, 0, 1280, -1, 1)
- `smoothstep = (e0: f64, e1: f64, x: f64) -> f64`
- `rk4[T] = (y: T, t: f64, dt: f64, rate: (T, f64) -> T) -> T`: the state y of a simulation dt seconds on, by the classic Runge-Kutta method (far closer than y + rate(y, t) * dt, Euler's: an orbit stays closed, a spring keeps its energy): rate(y, t) says how fast y changes at time t. y is a number, a vector, an f64[], or a struct with + and * by a number of its own ((+) = (a: S, b: S) -> S: ...)
- `rk4 = (y: f64[], t: f64, dt: f64, rate: (f64[], f64) -> f64[]) -> f64[]`
  ```gdscript
  # a pendulum: (angle, angular speed)
  s = rk4(s, t, dt, (s: vec2, t: f64) -> vec2: vec2(s.y, -9.81 / length * sin(s.x)))
  ```
- `sin = (x: f64) -> f64`
- `cos = (x: f64) -> f64`
- `tan = (x: f64) -> f64`
- `atan = (x0: f64) -> f64`
- `atan2 = (y: f64, x: f64) -> f64`
- `asin = (x: f64) -> f64`
- `acos = (x: f64) -> f64`
- `exp = (x: f64) -> f64`
- `tanh = (x: f64) -> f64`: the hyperbolic tangent: from -1 to 1, through 0 (a smooth limit: soft clipping, squashing)
- `log = (x: f64) -> f64`
- `log2 = (x: f64) -> f64`
- `log10 = (x: f64) -> f64`
- `pow = (x: f64, y: f64) -> f64`: x to the power y, within an ulp (fdlibm's: log2(x) in extra precision, times y, then 2 to that)
- `exp = (x: f32) -> f32`: f32 versions (shader code, games): computed in double with shorter polynomials, so they are within an ulp of the f32 result at a fraction of the cost of the full-precision ones
- `log = (x: f32) -> f32`
- `pow = (x: f32, y: f32) -> f32`
- `sin = (x: f32) -> f32`
- `cos = (x: f32) -> f32`
- `pow = (x: int, n: int) -> int`
- `f64_bits = (x: f64) -> u64`: a float's bits as an integer, and back (IEEE 754: f64_bits(1.0) is 0x3FF0000000000000)
- `f64_from_bits = (b: u64) -> f64`
- `f32_bits = (x: f32) -> u32`
- `f32_from_bits = (b: u32) -> f32`
- `hypot = (x: f64, y: f64) -> f64`
- `radians = (deg: f64) -> f64`
- `degrees = (rad: f64) -> f64`

#### Random numbers (xoshiro256**)

- `struct Rng`
  ```gdscript
  struct Rng:
      s0: u64 = 0x9E3779B97F4A7C15
      s1: u64 = 0xBF58476D1CE4E5B9
      s2: u64 = 0x94D049BB133111EB
      s3: u64 = 0x2545F4914F6CDD1D
  ```
- `rng = (seed: int) -> Rng`
- `next_u64 = (r: mut Rng) -> u64`
- `next_float = (r: mut Rng) -> f64`: uniform f64 in [0, 1)
- `next_normal = (r: mut Rng, mean: f64 = 0.0, sd: f64 = 1.0) -> f64`: normally distributed, from r: mean 0 and standard deviation 1 (as randn), or the ones given
- `next_int = (r: mut Rng, lo: int, hi: int) -> int`: uniform int in [lo, hi)
- `next_item[T] = (r: mut Rng, xs: T[]) -> T`: an element of xs (not empty), from r
- `shuffle[T] = (r: mut Rng, xs: mut T[])`: put xs in a random order from r
- `seed_random = (seed: int)`
- `random = () -> f64`: random f64 in [0, 1)
- `random = (lo: f64, hi: f64) -> f64`: random f64 in [lo, hi)
- `random_normal = (mean: f64 = 0.0, sd: f64 = 1.0) -> f64`: a random number from a bell curve around mean: about two in three within sd of it (p5's randomGaussian; Rng's next_normal for a seeded one)
- `random_int = (lo: int, hi: int) -> int`: a random whole number from lo up to hi, hi left out (as in lo..hi): random_int(0, xs.len()) picks a position, and a die is random_int(1, 7)
- `random_item[T] = (xs: T[]) -> T`: a random element of xs (which must not be empty)
- `shuffle[T] = (xs: mut T[])`: put xs in a random order

### integer.jo

Number theory on whole numbers, as contests and puzzles want it: greatest common divisors, square roots and arithmetic modulo m, exact for every int (no float in between):

```gdscript
gcd(12, 18)                  # 6
isqrt(10 ^ 18 - 1)           # 999999999
pow_mod(3, 10 ^ 18, 1_000_000_007)
inv_mod(2, 1_000_000_007)    # 500000004 (an optional: none when there is none)
mul_mod(a, b, m)             # a * b mod m without overflow, for m up to 2^63
```

The results of the modulo functions are from 0 to m - 1, negative arguments too (as `mod(x, m)`; `%` keeps the sign of x). Past 64 bits, BigInt keeps every digit, with gcd, isqrt, pow_mod and the others for it too (bigint.jo).

- `gcd = (a: int, b: int) -> int`: the greatest common divisor, 0 or more (gcd(0, 0) is 0; gcd(-4, 6) is 2)
- `gcd = (a: u64, b: u64) -> u64`
- `lcm = (a: int, b: int) -> int`: the least common multiple, 0 or more (0 when either is 0; it wraps around when it does not fit, as `*` does)
- `lcm = (a: u64, b: u64) -> u64`
- `isqrt = (n: int) -> int`: the whole square root: the largest r with r * r <= n (isqrt(15) is 3), exact where int(sqrt(f64(n))) is not (past 2^52)
- `mul_mod = (a: int, b: int, m: int) -> int`: a * b mod m, from 0 to m - 1, with the 128-bit product (a * b % m overflows once a * b passes 2^63, as it does for m past about 3e9)
- `mul_mod = (a: u64, b: u64, m: u64) -> u64`
- `pow_mod = (base: int, e: int, m: int) -> int`: base to the power e, mod m (from 0 to m - 1), in about log2(e) steps: pow_mod(2, 10 ^ 18, 1_000_000_007). A negative e is a power of base's inverse (pow_mod(2, -1, 7) is 4); without one it stops the program (inv_mod says whether there is one)
- `pow_mod = (base: u64, e: u64, m: u64) -> u64`
- `inv_mod = (a: int, m: int) -> int?`: the inverse of a mod m: the x from 0 to m - 1 with a * x mod m == 1, or none when a and m have a common divisor (inv_mod(3, 7) is 5; inv_mod(2, 4) is none). For a prime m it is also pow_mod(a, m - 2, m)
- `to_u64 = (s: str) -> u64?`: parse a whole number from 0 to 2^64 - 1 (to_int stops at 2^63 - 1), as to_int does: decimal, or hex after 0x, binary after 0b, octal after 0o; _ may separate digits; none if it is not one, is negative or does not fit
- `to_u64 = (s: str, base: int) -> u64?`: a whole number from 0 to 2^64 - 1 written in base 2 to 36 (to_u64("ffffffffffffffff", 16))

### checked.jo

Integer arithmetic that says when it overflows. `+`, `-` and `*` on integers wrap around, in debug and release builds alike (`i8(127) + i8(1)` is -128); these say what to do instead:

```gdscript
checked_add(a, b)        # an optional: none when the sum does not fit the type
saturating_add(a, b)     # the type's largest (or smallest) value when it does not fit
wrapping_add(a, b)       # as a + b: wrapped around, said on purpose
checked_cast[u8](x)      # x as a u8 when it fits, else none (u8(x) keeps the low bits)
saturating_cast[u8](x)   # 0 below, 255 above

total = checked_add(total, n) ?? panic("total overflowed")
if let p = checked_mul(w, h): ...
```

They take two values of one integer type (int, i32, i16, i8, u64, u32, u16, u8) and give that type; values of mixed types go as the int ones, as other operators do (`checked_add(x, n)` with x an i32 and n an int is an int). A literal takes the other's type: `checked_add(b, 1)` with b a u8 is a u8.

#### Checked: none when the exact result does not fit

- `checked_add = (a: int, b: int) -> int?`: a + b, or none when it overflows
- `checked_add = (a: u64, b: u64) -> u64?`
- `checked_add = (a: i32, b: i32) -> i32?`
- `checked_add = (a: i16, b: i16) -> i16?`
- `checked_add = (a: i8, b: i8) -> i8?`
- `checked_add = (a: u32, b: u32) -> u32?`
- `checked_add = (a: u16, b: u16) -> u16?`
- `checked_add = (a: u8, b: u8) -> u8?`
- `checked_sub = (a: int, b: int) -> int?`: a - b, or none when it overflows (for the unsigned types: when b > a)
- `checked_sub = (a: u64, b: u64) -> u64?`
- `checked_sub = (a: i32, b: i32) -> i32?`
- `checked_sub = (a: i16, b: i16) -> i16?`
- `checked_sub = (a: i8, b: i8) -> i8?`
- `checked_sub = (a: u32, b: u32) -> u32?`
- `checked_sub = (a: u16, b: u16) -> u16?`
- `checked_sub = (a: u8, b: u8) -> u8?`
- `checked_mul = (a: int, b: int) -> int?`: a * b, or none when it overflows
- `checked_mul = (a: u64, b: u64) -> u64?`
- `checked_mul = (a: i32, b: i32) -> i32?`
- `checked_mul = (a: i16, b: i16) -> i16?`
- `checked_mul = (a: i8, b: i8) -> i8?`
- `checked_mul = (a: u32, b: u32) -> u32?`
- `checked_mul = (a: u16, b: u16) -> u16?`
- `checked_mul = (a: u8, b: u8) -> u8?`

#### Saturating: the nearest value the type has

- `saturating_add = (a: int, b: int) -> int`: a + b, or the type's largest or smallest value when it overflows
- `saturating_add = (a: u64, b: u64) -> u64`
- `saturating_add = (a: i32, b: i32) -> i32`
- `saturating_add = (a: i16, b: i16) -> i16`
- `saturating_add = (a: i8, b: i8) -> i8`
- `saturating_add = (a: u32, b: u32) -> u32`
- `saturating_add = (a: u16, b: u16) -> u16`
- `saturating_add = (a: u8, b: u8) -> u8`
- `saturating_sub = (a: int, b: int) -> int`: a - b, or the type's largest or smallest value when it overflows (0 for the unsigned types when b > a)
- `saturating_sub = (a: u64, b: u64) -> u64`
- `saturating_sub = (a: i32, b: i32) -> i32`
- `saturating_sub = (a: i16, b: i16) -> i16`
- `saturating_sub = (a: i8, b: i8) -> i8`
- `saturating_sub = (a: u32, b: u32) -> u32`
- `saturating_sub = (a: u16, b: u16) -> u16`
- `saturating_sub = (a: u8, b: u8) -> u8`
- `saturating_mul = (a: int, b: int) -> int`: a * b, or the type's largest or smallest value when it overflows
- `saturating_mul = (a: u64, b: u64) -> u64`
- `saturating_mul = (a: i32, b: i32) -> i32`
- `saturating_mul = (a: i16, b: i16) -> i16`
- `saturating_mul = (a: i8, b: i8) -> i8`
- `saturating_mul = (a: u32, b: u32) -> u32`
- `saturating_mul = (a: u16, b: u16) -> u16`
- `saturating_mul = (a: u8, b: u8) -> u8`

#### Wrapping: as the operators, said on purpose

- `wrapping_add = (a: int, b: int) -> int`: a + b, wrapped around on overflow (as `+` does: this says it is meant)
- `wrapping_add = (a: u64, b: u64) -> u64`
- `wrapping_add = (a: i32, b: i32) -> i32`
- `wrapping_add = (a: i16, b: i16) -> i16`
- `wrapping_add = (a: i8, b: i8) -> i8`
- `wrapping_add = (a: u32, b: u32) -> u32`
- `wrapping_add = (a: u16, b: u16) -> u16`
- `wrapping_add = (a: u8, b: u8) -> u8`
- `wrapping_sub = (a: int, b: int) -> int`: a - b, wrapped around on overflow (as `-` does: this says it is meant)
- `wrapping_sub = (a: u64, b: u64) -> u64`
- `wrapping_sub = (a: i32, b: i32) -> i32`
- `wrapping_sub = (a: i16, b: i16) -> i16`
- `wrapping_sub = (a: i8, b: i8) -> i8`
- `wrapping_sub = (a: u32, b: u32) -> u32`
- `wrapping_sub = (a: u16, b: u16) -> u16`
- `wrapping_sub = (a: u8, b: u8) -> u8`
- `wrapping_mul = (a: int, b: int) -> int`: a * b, wrapped around on overflow (as `*` does: this says it is meant)
- `wrapping_mul = (a: u64, b: u64) -> u64`
- `wrapping_mul = (a: i32, b: i32) -> i32`
- `wrapping_mul = (a: i16, b: i16) -> i16`
- `wrapping_mul = (a: i8, b: i8) -> i8`
- `wrapping_mul = (a: u32, b: u32) -> u32`
- `wrapping_mul = (a: u16, b: u16) -> u16`
- `wrapping_mul = (a: u8, b: u8) -> u8`

#### Conversions

- `checked_cast[T] = (x: int) -> T?`: x as a T (an integer type: checked_cast[u8](x)) when it has that value, else none (`u8(x)` keeps the low bits: u8(300) is 44, checked_cast[u8](300) none)
- `checked_cast[T] = (x: u64) -> T?`
- `saturating_cast[T] = (x: int) -> T`: x as a T (an integer type: saturating_cast[u8](x)), or T's smallest or largest value when x is past it (saturating_cast[u8](300) is 255, saturating_cast[u8](-5) 0)
- `saturating_cast[T] = (x: u64) -> T`

## Big integers

`BigInt` is a whole number of any size, every digit kept, as Python's `int`: `bigint(2) ^ 1000`, `bigint("123456789012345678901234567890")`. The operators, comparisons and printing work as on ints, ints mixed in; it can be a map key or in a set. Nothing is imported (the compiler reads it for the programs that use it).

```gdscript
# example: exact big integers
f = bigint(1)
loop i in [1..50]: f *= i                # 50!, all 65 digits
print(f, len(str(f)))
x = bigint("123456789012345678901234567890")
print(x * x + 1, x / 7, x % 7, -x >> 3)
print(bigint(2) ^ 100 > x, digits(bigint(2) ^ 1000).sum(), str(bigint(255), 16))
print(gcd(f, bigint(2) ^ 100), isqrt(x), pow_mod(bigint(3), x, bigint(2) ^ 127 - 1))
print(int(x / bigint(10) ^ 20), to_int(x), to_bigint("0xffff_ffff_ffff_ffff_ff"))
```

Exact whole numbers of any size: 2 ^ 1000's 302 digits, factorials, Fibonacci(10000), RSA's modular arithmetic. Every digit is kept (Big, `use big`, is a float with a large exponent: about 15 digits, for an idle game's money).

```gdscript
f = bigint(1)
loop i in [1..100]: f *= i               # 100!, all 158 digits
print(f)
x = bigint("123456789012345678901234567890")
y = (x * x + 1) % bigint(10) ^ 20
if y > x: print(str(y, 16))              # in hex
print(pow_mod(bigint(3), bigint(10) ^ 30, bigint(2) ^ 127 - 1))
```

`+ - * / %` and `^` (an int power) work as on ints, with BigInts and ints mixed (`x * 2`, `1 + x`); `/` truncates toward zero and `%` keeps the sign of the left side, as for ints (`-7 / 2` is -3, `-7 % 3` is -1; `mod(x, m)` is from 0 to m - 1). Comparisons, `==`, `min`, `max` and `sort` work, and a BigInt can be a map key or in a set. `print` shows its digits; `int(x)` or `to_int(x)` give it back as an int. to_text, to_json and saves write its fields, which read back.

- `struct BigInt`
- `bigint = (n: int) -> BigInt`: n as a BigInt
- `bigint = (n: u64) -> BigInt`
- `bigint = (s: str) -> BigInt`: the whole number written in text, as to_bigint reads it; text that is not one stops the program (to_bigint says none instead)
- `to_bigint = (s: str) -> BigInt?`: the whole number written in text: decimal digits, or hex after 0x, binary after 0b, octal after 0o, with a sign before it if any; _ may separate digits, and spaces and line ends around it are left out (as to_int reads one, of any length). none if it is not one
- `to_bigint = (s: str, base: int) -> BigInt?`: a whole number written in base 2 to 36 (to_bigint("ff", 16) is 255; letters for the digits past 9, in either case; the base's own prefix may come first: "0xff")
- `to_int = (x: BigInt) -> int?`: x as an int, or none when it does not fit (from -2^63 to 2^63 - 1)
- `int = (x: BigInt) -> int`: x as an int; one that does not fit stops the program (to_int says none instead)
- `to_u64 = (x: BigInt) -> u64?`: x as a u64, or none when it is negative or past 2^64 - 1
- `f64 = (x: BigInt) -> f64`: the f64 nearest x (infinity past about 1.8e308)
- `str = (x: BigInt, base: int) -> str`: x shown in base 2 to 36, with lowercase letters for the digits past 9 (str(bigint(255), 16) is "ff"; a minus sign first when it is negative)
- `str = (x: BigInt) -> str`: x in decimal: print(x) and "{x}" show it so
- `digits = (x: BigInt) -> int[]`: the decimal digits of x, most significant first, without its sign (digits(bigint(2) ^ 10) is [1, 0, 2, 4]; for 0, [0]): digits(bigint(2) ^ 1000).sum()
- `bit_length = (x: BigInt) -> int`: how many bits x takes without its sign: bit_length(bigint(255)) is 8; 0 for 0
- `sign = (x: BigInt) -> int`: -1, 0 or 1
- `abs = (x: BigInt) -> BigInt`
- `(-) = (a: BigInt) -> BigInt`
- `(+) = (a: BigInt, b: BigInt) -> BigInt`
- `(-) = (a: BigInt, b: BigInt) -> BigInt`
- `(*) = (a: BigInt, b: BigInt) -> BigInt`
- `(/) = (a: BigInt, b: BigInt) -> BigInt`: truncated toward zero, as for ints (bigint(-7) / 2 is -3)
- `(%) = (a: BigInt, b: BigInt) -> BigInt`: the remainder of a / b, with a's sign, as for ints (bigint(-7) % 3 is -1; mod(x, m) is from 0 to m - 1)
- `pow = (x: BigInt, e: int) -> BigInt`: x to the power e (x ^ e). A negative e truncates as / does: 0 unless x is 1 or -1
- `(==) = (a: BigInt, b: BigInt) -> bool`
- `(<) = (a: BigInt, b: BigInt) -> bool`
- `hash = (x: BigInt) -> u64`: a number made from x: the same for BigInts that are ==, so a BigInt can be a key

#### Mixed with ints

- `(+) = (a: BigInt, b: int) -> BigInt`
- `(+) = (a: int, b: BigInt) -> BigInt`
- `(-) = (a: BigInt, b: int) -> BigInt`
- `(-) = (a: int, b: BigInt) -> BigInt`
- `(*) = (a: BigInt, b: int) -> BigInt`
- `(*) = (a: int, b: BigInt) -> BigInt`
- `(/) = (a: BigInt, b: int) -> BigInt`
- `(/) = (a: int, b: BigInt) -> BigInt`
- `(%) = (a: BigInt, b: int) -> BigInt`
- `(%) = (a: int, b: BigInt) -> BigInt`
- `(==) = (a: BigInt, b: int) -> bool`
- `(==) = (a: int, b: BigInt) -> bool`
- `(<) = (a: BigInt, b: int) -> bool`
- `(<) = (a: int, b: BigInt) -> bool`
- `(<<) = (x: BigInt, n: int) -> BigInt`: x shifted left n bits: x * 2 ^ n
- `(>>) = (x: BigInt, n: int) -> BigInt`: x shifted right n bits, rounding down as >> on ints does (bigint(-7) >> 1 is -4)

#### Number theory

- `mod = (x: BigInt, m: BigInt) -> BigInt`: x mod m, from 0 to m - 1 for m > 0 (with m's sign, as Python's %: mod(bigint(-7), 3) is 2)
- `mod = (x: BigInt, m: int) -> BigInt`
- `gcd = (a: BigInt, b: BigInt) -> BigInt`: the greatest common divisor, 0 or more (gcd of 0 and 0 is 0)
- `lcm = (a: BigInt, b: BigInt) -> BigInt`: the least common multiple, 0 or more (0 when either is 0)
- `isqrt = (n: BigInt) -> BigInt`: the whole square root: the largest r with r * r <= n
- `inv_mod = (a: BigInt, m: BigInt) -> BigInt?`: the inverse of a mod m: the x from 0 to m - 1 with a * x mod m == 1, or none when a and m have a common divisor
- `pow_mod = (base: BigInt, e: BigInt, m: BigInt) -> BigInt`: base to the power e, mod m (from 0 to m - 1), in about log2(e) steps: RSA's pow_mod(msg, e, n). A negative e is a power of base's inverse; without one it stops the program
- `pow_mod = (base: BigInt, e: int, m: BigInt) -> BigInt`

## Files, time and the system

In a web page, files are the ones the page was given, and `env` reads its address's parameters. What is at a web address: `net.fetch(url)`, after `import net` ([fetching from the web](START.md#fetching-from-the-web)).

```gdscript
# example: files, time and the system
start = time()
write_file("notes.txt", "first line\nsecond line\n")
if let text = read_file("notes.txt"):    # none if it could not be read
    loop line in text.lines(): print("> {line}")
print(file_exists("notes.txt"), args())
delete_file("notes.txt")
docs = documents_dir()                    # (C:\Users\me\Documents, /home/me/Documents)
print(home_dir(), join_path(docs, "report.txt"), cpu_count())
print("took {(time() - start) * 1000.0:.2} ms")
```

### os.jo

Operating system access: files, arguments, environment, time.

- `exit = (code: int)`
- `args = () -> str[]`: command line arguments (args()[0] is the program as it was started: `sloppy tool.jo` gives "tool.jo")
- `env = (name: str) -> str?`
- `file_error = () -> str`: why the last file operation that failed did, in words: "no such file or directory", "permission denied", "it is a directory", "the disk is full"... ("" when none has failed):
  ```gdscript
  if not write_file(p, text): eprint("cannot write {p}: {file_error()}")
  ```
- `read_file = (path: str) -> str?`: read a whole file; none if it cannot be read (or is a directory: file_error() says why). A UTF-8 byte order mark at its start (Notepad's "UTF-8 with BOM") is left out; read_bytes keeps every byte
- `read_line = () -> str?`: a line typed in (or piped in), without its end; none when the input has ended
- `struct File`: A file open for reading a piece at a time (a log too big to load whole, the start of a file to hash):
  ```gdscript
  if let f = open_file("big.log"):
      loop let line = f.read_line(): ...
      f.close()
  ```
  ```gdscript
  struct File:
      handle = -1
      buf: u8[]
      pos = 0
      done = false
      path = ""                   # (made by create_file, in a web page: where its pieces go)
  ```
- `create_file = (path: str) -> File?`: A file made (or emptied) for writing a piece at a time (a download, a log written as it goes):
  ```gdscript
  if let f = create_file("out.bin"):
      f.write(piece)
      f.close()
  ```
- `write = (f: mut File, b: u8[]) -> bool`: add to a file made by create_file (false when it could not: file_error says why)
- `write = (f: mut File, s: str) -> bool`
- `open_file = (path: str) -> File?`
- `read_line = (f: mut File) -> str?`: the next line of the file, without its end (none past the last)
- `read_bytes = (f: mut File, n: int) -> u8[]`: up to n more bytes of the file (fewer at its end; none left: empty)
- `close = (f: mut File)`
- `input = (prompt: str = "") -> str`: a line typed by the user, after showing the prompt (Python's input): "" once the input ends. In a web page, a box asks for it.
- `input_int = (prompt: str = "") -> int`: a whole number typed by the user, asked again until it is one
- `input_float = (prompt: str = "") -> f64`: a number (12, 2.5) typed by the user, asked again until it is one
- `read_stdin = () -> str`: all of standard input (what is piped in), until it ends
- `read_bytes = (path: str) -> u8[]?`
- `page_url = () -> str`: a web page's own address, without its ?query (to make a link to it: page_url() + "?invite=" + code); "" in a native program
- `download_file = (path: str, name: str = "") -> bool`: a file the program wrote, given to the person using it: in a web page, the browser downloads it (a picture saved with save_png, an exported save), named `name` or as the path ends; elsewhere it is already where it was written. True if the file is there
- `write_file = (path: str, contents: str) -> bool`
- `write_bytes = (path: str, b: u8[]) -> bool`
- `is_dir = (path: str) -> bool`: a directory (and not a file)
- `is_symlink = (path: str) -> bool`: the path is a symbolic link (to anything, or to nothing); is_dir and the rest follow links, so a walk through directories that should not go round in circles asks this first
- `file_size = (path: str) -> int?`: a file's size in bytes; none if it cannot be opened (or is a directory)
- `file_exists = (path: str) -> bool`
- `make_executable = (path: str) -> bool`
- `delete_file = (path: str) -> bool`
- `time = () -> f64`: seconds since an arbitrary point (monotonic clock): the one to measure how long code takes (elapsed() is the game's clock, which a screenshot run advances 1/60 s a frame)
- `time_ns = () -> int`: nanoseconds since an arbitrary point (monotonic clock)
- `sleep = (seconds: f64)`
- `write = (s: str)`: print without a trailing newline
- `flush_output = ()`: what print and write gave so far, written out now (output to a file or a pipe is collected and written in large pieces; to a terminal it goes at once anyway)
- `is_terminal = () -> bool`: standard output is a terminal (not a file or a pipe)
- `eprint = (s: str)`: print to standard error (for messages about the program, kept apart from its output), and a line break; it takes what print takes: eprint("bad line", i, xs)
- `list_dir = (path: str) -> str[]`: names of the entries in a directory (excluding . and ..); [] for a file or a path that is not there too (is_dir tells them apart)
- `exe_path = () -> str`: absolute path of the running executable (a script run with `sloppy tool.jo` is built into a cache first: its own path is args()[0])
- `exec = (path: str, argv: str[]) -> bool`: replace the current process with another program (returns only on failure)
- `set_env = (name: str, value: str)`: an environment variable set, for this program and the programs it starts after this
- `unset_env = (name: str)`: an environment variable taken away, for this program and the programs it starts after this
- `env_all = () -> {str: str}`: every environment variable, by name (in a web page: only those set with set_env)
- `spawn_detached = (path: str, argv: str[]) -> bool`: start a program in the background, detached from this one: its own session, no terminal (input and output go to /dev/null); returns whether it was started
- `run_program = (program: str, args: str[] = [], show: bool = false, cwd: str = "") -> (int, str)`: run a program and wait for it to end: its exit code (-1: it could not be started) and what it wrote to its standard output (its errors go where this program's go). A program named without a / is looked for on the PATH. args are its arguments, after its name. show = true: its output goes straight to this program's as it runs (a build's progress), and the text returned is "". cwd: the directory it runs in ("": this program's; a program named with a relative path is found from there). In a program with tasks the others take their turns meanwhile (on Windows they wait for it), and a task cancelled meanwhile ends it (its code is then 137)
- `run_shell = (command: str, show: bool = false, cwd: str = "") -> (int, str)`: run a command line through the system's shell (sh; cmd on Windows): pipes, `*.jo`, `&&` work as typed. Its exit code and what it wrote to its standard output; show and cwd as run_program's
- `chmod = (path: str, mode: int) -> bool`
- `mkdir = (path: str) -> bool`
- `make_dirs = (path: str) -> bool`: make a directory and the ones it is in ("out/maps/2"); true if it is there after
- `delete_dir = (path: str) -> bool`: remove a directory, which must be empty (delete_file for a file)
- `rename_file = (from: str, to: str, replace: bool = true) -> bool`: move or rename a file or directory (one already at `to` is replaced: a file, or an empty directory). replace = false: false instead when something is at `to` (file_error() says "it is already there"), checked as it moves, so no other program can put one there in between
- `copy_file = (from: str, to: str) -> bool`: copy a file's contents to another (made, or replaced)
- `append_file = (path: str, contents: str) -> bool`: add to the end of a file (made if it is not there): a log
- `file_time = (path: str) -> f64?`: when a file or directory was last changed, in seconds since 1970 (as unix_time()); none if it is not there
- `walk_dir = (path: str) -> str[]`: every file under a directory, at any depth, as paths from it ("maps/level1.tmx"), sorted; links to directories are not followed
- `glob = (pattern: str) -> str[]`: the files whose paths match a pattern: `*` is any part of a name, `?` one character, and `**` any number of directories ("src/*.jo", "assets/**/*.png"), sorted. As in the shell, hidden names (.git, .env) match only a pattern that starts them with a dot (".*", "**/.env"). On Windows letters match in either case, as its file names do ("*.TXT" matches notes.txt); elsewhere only in the same case. On Windows a pattern's `\` is a `/` ("data\*.txt" gives data/notes.txt)
- `memory_in_use = () -> int`: the memory this program has in use, in bytes (what the system counts as its resident set: on Linux, its pages in RAM; on Windows, its working set); 0 where it is not known (macOS, a web page)
- `cwd = () -> str`: the current directory
- `temp_dir = () -> str`: a directory for temporary files (TMPDIR, else /tmp; on Windows, the user's temporary directory)

### path.jo

Paths as text: their parts, and joining them (nothing is looked up on the disk). Both / and \ separate (Windows takes either).

```gdscript
file_name("art/hero.png") is "hero.png", dir_name(...) "art", extension(...) ".png",
without_extension(...) "art/hero", join_path("art", "hero.png") "art/hero.png"
```

- `file_name = (path: str) -> str`: the last part: the file's name ("" for a path ending in a separator)
- `dir_name = (path: str) -> str`: all but the last part ("." for a bare name; "/" for one at the root)
- `extension = (path: str) -> str`: the file name's extension with its dot (".png"; "" when it has none, or is like ".bashrc")
- `without_extension = (path: str) -> str`: the path without its file name's extension ("art/hero.png" -> "art/hero")
- `join_path = (a: str, b: str) -> str`: a then b, with one separator between them (b as it is when it starts at the root). The separator is /, but on Windows \ when a is written with \ or is a drive ("C:\Users\me" and "report.txt" give "C:\Users\me\report.txt"; "art" and "hero.png" give "art/hero.png")
- `home_dir = () -> str`: the user's home directory ("" when there is none: in a web page): /home/me, C:\Users\me
- `documents_dir = () -> str`: the user's Documents folder ("" when there is none: in a web page). On Windows wherever it is (OneDrive may hold it); on Linux XDG_DOCUMENTS_DIR from ~/.config/user-dirs.dirs, else ~/Documents; on macOS ~/Documents. It may not be there yet: make_dirs makes it
- `abs_path = (path: str) -> str`: the path from the root: a relative one from the working directory, with `.` and `..` worked out ("../x" in /home/me/src is /home/me/x; links are not followed). On Windows, with \

### date.jo

Dates and the wall clock: what time it is (unix_time), and the calendar date and time of day of a moment, where the computer is (its time zone) or in UTC.

```gdscript
d = now()
print("{d.year}-{d.month:02}-{d.day:02} {d.hour:02}:{d.minute:02}")   # 2026-10-14 08:15
print(date_text(now()))                                               # 2026-10-14 08:15:03
started = unix_time()  ...  print("took {unix_time() - started:.1} s")
```

(time() is the clock to measure with in a game: it never jumps, as the wall clock may)

- `struct Date`
  ```gdscript
  struct Date:
      year: int
      month: int          # 1 (January) to 12
      day: int            # 1 to 31
      hour: int           # 0 to 23
      minute: int         # 0 to 59
      second: int         # 0 to 59
      weekday: int        # 0 (Monday) to 6 (Sunday)
      offset: int         # its time zone: seconds east of UTC (0: UTC; New York in winter: -18000)
  ```
- `unix_time = () -> f64`: seconds since 1970-01-01 00:00 UTC, now (the wall clock: it can jump when it is set)
- `date = (t: f64, utc: bool = false) -> Date`: the date and time of day of moment t (unix seconds), in the computer's time zone, or in UTC
- `now = () -> Date`: the date and time of day it is now, where the computer is
- `unix_time = (d: Date) -> f64`: the moment (unix seconds) a date stands for (its offset says where it was: from date(), or 0 for a Date made as UTC); fields past their range carry over (month 13 is January of the next year)
- `parse_date = (text: str, utc: bool = false) -> Date?`: a date written as ISO 8601 has it, or none: "2026-10-14", "2026-10-14 08:15", "2026-10-14T08:15:03Z" (Z: UTC), "2026-10-14T08:15:03.250+02:00". One without a time zone is where the computer is (utc = true: in UTC)
- `date_text = (d: Date) -> str`: "2026-10-14 08:15:03"
- `month_name = (d: Date) -> str`: the names of a date's month and weekday, in English ("October", "Wednesday")
- `weekday_name = (d: Date) -> str`
- `utc_offset = (t: int) -> int`: how many seconds east of UTC the computer's clock is at moment t (unix seconds): its time zone and daylight saving time then (the TZ variable, or the system's zone)

### cli.jo

A command line's options, read into a struct: its fields are the options, their defaults what an option left out keeps.

```gdscript
struct Options:
    verbose = false         # say each file's name
    out = "report.txt"      # where the counts go
    jobs = 4                # files read at once
    files: str[]            # the files to count

opts = parse_args[Options]("count lines in files")
```

That reads --verbose (or -v), --out file.txt (--out=file.txt, -o file.txt), --jobs 8 (a whole number) and the arguments that are not options (into the last list field). `--help` (or -h, and on Windows /?) prints what the options are, each with the comment written beside its field (or on the lines just above it), and ends the program; a mistake (an option it does not have, a word where a number goes) is said with the same help, and ends it with exit code 2. A field `dry_run` is --dry-run; -x is the one field starting with x (when only one does; -h is help, so a field starting with h is -H). A list field given as an option takes it each time (--tag a --tag b). Enums take a variant's name (--mode slow), optionals a value or nothing. After `--` all are arguments.

- `parse_args[T] = (about: str = "") -> T`: the program's command line (after its name) read into a T, as above. about: one line on what the program does, for --help
- `read_args[T] = (words: str[]) -> T \| str`: words of a command line read into a T, or what is wrong with them (parse_args without its --help, messages and exit: for tests, or a program's own handling)
- `args_help[T] = (program: str, about: str = "") -> str`: what --help shows: the usage line, about, and each option with its default

### stop.jo

Stopping a program that runs until it is stopped (a server, a tool watching something): ctrl-c in its terminal, SIGTERM (what systemd, docker stop and kill send) and, on Windows, ctrl-break or its console closed ask it to stop. A program that cleans up first watches for that:

```gdscript
loop not stopping():
    ...
    net.wait(1.0)              # (or sleep: either ends early when it is asked)
save()
```

or has an on_quit = (): ..., which runs as any program ends: its window closed, its last line done, exit() called, or asked to stop. A program that does not look at stopping() ends at its next wait (sleep, net.wait, a server's), running on_quit; one busy elsewhere, at a second ctrl-c or SIGTERM (then on_quit does not run).

With tasks (lib/std/task.jo), the sleep or net.wait of a task that has asked stopping() ends early as well, and so does a select with a `stopping():` arm. The usual order: main (or the server's loop) sees stopping(), tells its tasks (closing the channels they read, or t.cancel()), waits for them, then saves. A task that goes on is told after a moment, with where it waits; a second ctrl-c ends the program.

- `stopping = () -> bool`: true once the program has been asked to stop (ctrl-c, SIGTERM, its console closed): from the first call on, being asked no longer ends the program at once, it is for the program to stop

## Terminal programs

A module (`import term`): the whole terminal as a screen of characters, as curses, tcell or bubbletea give it: keys (with ctrl, alt and shift), the mouse, resizes and pasted text as events, colors and styles, boxes, and a flush that writes only the cells that changed. The terminal is put back as it was however the program ends. See [terminal programs](LANGUAGE.md#terminal-programs) and [examples/files.jo](https://github.com/david-andrew/sloppy-lang/blob/master/examples/files.jo).

```gdscript
# example: a terminal program
# an @ moved by the arrows, or put where the mouse clicks; a clock ticking in a task meanwhile
import term

x = 10
y = 5
ticks = 0

show = ():
    w, h = term.size()
    term.clear()
    term.box(0, 0, w, h - 1, fg = term.CYAN, lines = .rounded, title = "arrows or the mouse move it, q quits")
    term.put(x, y, "@", fg = term.YELLOW, style = term.BOLD)
    x2 = term.put(0, h - 1, "at {x}, {y}  ", fg = term.GRAY)
    term.put(x2, h - 1, "{ticks / 10}.{ticks % 10} s", fg = term.color(rgb(1, 0.5, 0)))
    term.flush()

# (a task: it runs while main waits for a key)
clock = ():
    loop:
        sleep(0.1)
        ticks += 1
        show()

main = () -> int:
    global x, y
    if not term.start(mouse = true):
        eprint("not a terminal")
        return 1
    spawn(clock, background = true)
    loop:
        show()
        e = term.read_event() ?? break
        if e.kind == .mouse and e.mouse == .press:
            x = e.x
            y = e.y
        match e.name:
            "q", "escape": break
            "left": x -= 1
            "right": x += 1
            "up": y -= 1
            "down": y += 1
    0
```

Terminal programs: the whole terminal as a screen of characters, drawn into and shown (a file browser, an editor, a game in the terminal), as curses, tcell or bubbletea do. `import term`:

```gdscript
term.start()                            # keys one at a time, a screen of the program's own
loop:
    term.clear()
    term.box(0, 0, 30, 5, title = "hello")
    term.put(2, 2, "q quits", fg = term.YELLOW, style = term.BOLD)
    term.flush()                        # what changed since the last flush, in one write
    if let e = term.read_event():       # a key, the mouse, a resize, text pasted
        if e.name == "q" or e.name == "escape": break
```

Drawing goes into a screen of cells (a character, its colors and style) that flush compares with what the terminal shows, writing only the cells that changed. Characters of East Asian scripts and emoji take two cells. Columns (x) and rows (y) count from 0 at the top left.

The terminal is put back as it was (its screen, the cursor, keys echoed and read by line) by term.stop(), and as the program ends whichever way: its end, exit(), a panic or a crash, ctrl-c, SIGTERM or the terminal closed. Ctrl-c asks the program to stop as it does elsewhere (stopping() and on_quit, lib/std/stop.jo): a program that does not look ends at its next read_event, flush or sleep. start(ctrl_c_key = true) makes ctrl-c a key the program reads ("ctrl+c") instead. Ctrl-z and ctrl-\ are keys too.

In a program with tasks, a task waiting in read_event lets the others run (on Windows it looks for input every 10 ms meanwhile). Windows: keys, the mouse and resizes come from the console's events (paste comes as keys typed); a web page has no terminal (start gives false).

#### Colors and styles

- `const DEFAULT = 0`: Colors are numbers: DEFAULT is the terminal's own, the 16 named ones follow the terminal's theme, color(n) is one of the 256 of xterm's palette, and color(rgb(...)) any (24-bit).
- `const BLACK = 0x100`
- `const RED = 0x101`
- `const GREEN = 0x102`
- `const YELLOW = 0x103`
- `const BLUE = 0x104`
- `const MAGENTA = 0x105`
- `const CYAN = 0x106`
- `const WHITE = 0x107`
- `const GRAY = 0x108`
- `const BRIGHT_RED = 0x109`
- `const BRIGHT_GREEN = 0x10A`
- `const BRIGHT_YELLOW = 0x10B`
- `const BRIGHT_BLUE = 0x10C`
- `const BRIGHT_MAGENTA = 0x10D`
- `const BRIGHT_CYAN = 0x10E`
- `const BRIGHT_WHITE = 0x10F`
- `const BOLD = 1`: styles, joined with |: BOLD | UNDERLINE
- `const DIM = 2`
- `const ITALIC = 4`
- `const UNDERLINE = 8`
- `const REVERSE = 16`
- `const STRIKE = 32`
- `color = (n: int) -> int`: one of the 256 colors of xterm's palette: 0-15 the named ones, 16-231 a 6x6x6 cube (16 + 36 r + 6 g + b), 232-255 grays
- `color = (c: vec4) -> int`: a color of 24 bits, from one of the library's (rgb(1, 0.5, 0), hex(0xff8000), ORANGE)

#### Events

- `enum EventKind: key, mouse, resize, paste`
- `enum Key: char, enter, tab, backspace, delete, escape, up, down, left, right, home, end, page_up, page_down, insert, f1, f2, f3, f4, f5, f6, f7, f8, f9, f10, f11, f12`: a key: a character typed is .char (the character in the event's text)
- `enum Mouse: press, release, drag, move, wheel_up, wheel_down, wheel_left, wheel_right`: what the mouse did
- `struct Event`: what read_event gives: a key pressed, the mouse (start(mouse = true)), the terminal resized (the screen has its new size already, and shows it all again at the next flush), or text pasted
  ```gdscript
  struct Event:
      kind: EventKind
      key: Key                # a key's (.char: a character, in text)
      text = ""               # the character typed (a letter with ctrl is small: "c"), or the text pasted
      name = ""               # a key as text: "a", "A", "space", "enter", "ctrl+c", "alt+x", "shift+tab", "ctrl+up", "f5"
      ctrl = false
      alt = false
      shift = false           # (keys other than characters: a character typed with shift is that character, "A")
      mouse: Mouse            # the mouse's: what it did
      button = 0              # which button: 1 left, 2 middle, 3 right (0: none, as for a move or the wheel)
      x = 0                   # the mouse's column; resize: the new width
      y = 0                   # the mouse's row; resize: the new height
  ```
- `str = (e: Event) -> str`: an event as text: "key ctrl+c", "mouse press 1 at 10,3", "resize 120x40", "paste 12 bytes"

#### The terminal

- `start = (mouse: bool = false, mouse_moves: bool = false, ctrl_c_key: bool = false) -> bool`: the program takes the terminal over: keys come one at a time and are not shown, its screen is one of its own (the terminal's is back as it was afterwards), the cursor hidden, pasted text one event. mouse: the mouse's presses, releases, drags and wheel come as events (mouse_moves: its moves with no button held too). ctrl_c_key: ctrl-c is a key, not a request to stop. False when standard input or output is not a terminal (a pipe, a file); true if started already
- `stop = ()`: the terminal put back as it was before start (its screen, the cursor, its settings); start takes it over again (around run_program(..., show = true) of an editor, say)
- `size = () -> (int, int)`: the screen's size: its columns and rows (the terminal's, from start on; 80 x 24 before)
- `set_size = (w: int, h: int)`: the screen's size in cells, which start and a resize set: for tests without a terminal. What is drawn stays where it fits, and the next flush shows it all
- `set_title = (s: str)`: the terminal window's title

#### Reading events

- `read_event = (wait: f64 = -1.0) -> Event?`: the next key pressed, mouse event, resize or paste, waiting at most `wait` seconds for it (below 0: until one comes). none: none came in that time, the task waiting was cancelled, the terminal was closed, or the program has been asked to stop (ctrl-c: once, for a program that looks at stopping()). A lone escape is told from a key's sequence by 50 ms with nothing after it
- `decode = (b: u8[]) -> Event[]`: the events in a terminal's input bytes, as read_event makes them (an escape at the end is the key): for tests, and for a terminal's bytes from elsewhere

#### The screen

- `clear = (fg: int = 0, bg: int = 0)`: every cell blank, in the colors given (bg: the background all over)
- `put = (x: int, y: int, text: str, fg: int = 0, bg: int = 0, style: int = 0) -> int`: write text at column x of row y, going right; what is off the screen is left out (x may be below 0). The column after it: put(put(x, y, "name: "), y, name, fg = CYAN)
- `fill_rect = (x: int, y: int, w: int, h: int, ch: str = " ", fg: int = 0, bg: int = 0, style: int = 0)`: fill a rectangle with a character (a space: the background color alone)
- `enum Lines: single, double, rounded, heavy`: the lines a box is drawn with
- `box = (x: int, y: int, w: int, h: int, fg: int = 0, bg: int = 0, style: int = 0, lines: Lines = .single, title: str = "")`: a box's outline, with lines drawing characters: w columns wide, h rows high (the inside is left as it is: fill_rect for a background). title: written on its top line
- `hline = (x: int, y: int, w: int, fg: int = 0, bg: int = 0, style: int = 0, ch: str = "─")`: a line across, w columns long, from x (ch: what it is drawn with)
- `vline = (x: int, y: int, h: int, fg: int = 0, bg: int = 0, style: int = 0, ch: str = "│")`: a line down, h rows long, from y
- `text_width = (text: str) -> int`: how many columns text takes (two for a wide character, none for a mark on the one before)
- `fit = (text: str, width: int) -> str`: text cut to fit `width` columns (with … at its end when it is cut)
- `set_cursor = (x: int, y: int)`: show the cursor at column x of row y after each flush (where the user types)
- `hide_cursor = ()`: no cursor shown (as from start on)
- `redraw = ()`: the next flush shows every cell, not only those changed (after something else wrote to the terminal: a program run, a message)
- `flush = () -> int`: show what was drawn: the cells changed since the last flush, written in one go with the cursor moved as little as it can be. How many bytes were written (none while stopped)
- `render = () -> str`: what flush would write now, as text, the screen then counting it as shown: for tests without a terminal
- `screen_text = () -> str`: the screen's characters, a line for each row (colors and styles left out): for tests

## JSON, CSV, URLs, base64 and SHA-256

`to_json(value)` and `from_json[T](text)` turn any value (a struct, an array, a map...) into JSON and back; `parse_json` reads JSON of any shape as a `Json` to look inside.

```gdscript
# example: JSON, CSV, URLs, base64 and SHA-256
struct Monster:
    name = ""
    hp = 0
    drops: str[]

# a struct (or any value) to JSON and back
m = Monster("slime", 12, ["gel", "coin"])
text = to_json(m)
print(text)
back = from_json[Monster](text)
if back is Monster: print(back.name, back.hp)

# JSON of any shape: parse it and look inside
r = parse_json(r'{"name": "bat", "drops": ["wing"]}')
if r is Json:
    j: Json? = r
    print(j.get("name").as_str() ?? "?", j.get("drops").at(0).as_str() ?? "?")

# CSV: rows of fields
rows = csv_rows("name,hp\nslime,12\n\"big, bad bat\",40\n")
loop row in rows[1..]: print(row[0], to_int(row[1]) ?? 0)
print(trim(to_csv([["a", "b, c"]])))

# a URL's query, and text from a web page
print("https://example.com/search?" + url_query({"q": "café au lait", "page": "2"}))
print(html_unescape("Tom &amp; Jerry &mdash; &eacute;t&eacute;"))

code = base64_encode(bytes("hello"))
print(code, str_from_bytes(base64_decode(code) ?? []))
print(sha256("hello"))                   # a fingerprint, as sha256sum gives
```

### json.jo

JSON: parse_json reads text into a Json value (or a JsonError saying where it is wrong), and to_json writes one. Reading: get (a field), at (an element) and the as_* functions all take a Json? and give an optional, so they chain, and a missing piece anywhere gives none:

```gdscript
j = parse_json(text)
if j is JsonError: return j
width = j.get("width").as_int() ?? 0
name = j.get("layers").at(0).get("name").as_str() ?? "?"
loop l in j.get("layers").as_list(): print(l.get("name").as_str() ?? "")
```

For JSON straight into your own structs and back, from_json[Config](text) and to_json(value) are in "Saving, and values as text" (they give a TextError, with the same message, line, column).

- `enum Json: null_, boolean(b: bool), integer(i: int), number(n: f64), text(s: str), list(items: Json[]), object(keys: str[], values: Json[])`: a whole number in the text is an integer, kept exactly as written while it fits in an int; any other is a number (as_num and as_int read both). print shows a Json as JSON text
- `error struct JsonError`
  ```gdscript
  error struct JsonError:
      message: str
      line: int
      column: int
  ```
- `parse_json = (s: str) -> Json \| JsonError`

#### Reading

- `get = (j: Json?, key: str) -> Json?`: an object's field
- `at = (j: Json?, i: int) -> Json?`: a list's element
- `as_num = (j: Json?) -> f64?`
- `as_int = (j: Json?) -> int?`: (a number with a fraction: its whole part)
- `as_str = (j: Json?) -> str?`
- `as_bool = (j: Json?) -> bool?`
- `as_list = (j: Json?) -> Json[]`: a list's elements (none: an empty array)
- `keys = (j: Json?) -> str[]`: an object's field names, in order
- `is_null = (j: Json?) -> bool`

#### Writing

- `to_json = (j: Json, indent: int = 0) -> str`: JSON text for a value (indent > 0: on several lines, indented by that many spaces)
- `to_json[T] = (x: T, indent: int = 0) -> str`: any value as JSON: a struct as an object (by field name), an array, tuple or vector as a list, a map as an object (its keys as text), none as null, an enum's variant as its name (one with fields as {"name": [fields]}), and a Json inside it as itself; from_json[T] reads it back
- `json_value[T] = (x: T) -> Json`: any value as a Json (what to_json writes for it)

### csv.jo

CSV: rows of fields separated by commas (or another separator), as spreadsheets save them. A field in double quotes may hold the separator, line breaks and "" for a quote.

```gdscript
rows = csv_rows(read_file("sales.csv") ?? "")
loop row in rows[1..]: total += to_float(row[2]) ?? 0.0      # (rows[0]: the header)
```

Or each row as a struct, its fields found by the header's names:

```gdscript
struct Sale:
    date = ""
    region = ""
    price = 0.0          # (a number: checked; empty or NA is NaN)
    units = 0
    discount: f64? = none   # (empty or NA: none)
sales = from_csv[Sale](read_file("sales.csv") ?? "")
if sales is TextError: fail(sales.message)
```

- `csv_rows = (text: str, sep: u8 = ',') -> str[][]`: the rows of a CSV text, each the list of its fields (a header is the first row, like the others)
- `to_csv = (rows: str[][], sep: u8 = ',') -> str`: CSV text of the rows: a field with the separator, a quote or a line break is put in quotes
- `from_csv[T] = (text: str, sep: u8 = ',') -> T[] \| TextError`: the rows of a CSV text with a header, each as a T: a column goes to the field of its name ("Unit Price" to unit_price; others are skipped), a field with no column keeps its default. A number is checked (a TextError says the row, after the header, and column where one is not); an empty one, or NA, is NaN in an f64 and none in an optional

### url.jo

Text for the web: URLs taken apart and links followed from a page, a URL's parts percent-encoded and back, query strings, cookies, and HTML's entities.

```gdscript
url = "https://api.example.com/search?" + url_query({"q": "café au lait", "page": "2"})
name = url_decode("caf%C3%A9")                        # café
u = parse_url("https://example.com:8080/a/b?q=1")?   # u.host "example.com", u.port 8080
next = url_join("https://example.com/a/b/page.html", "../c.html")   # https://example.com/a/c.html
title = html_unescape("Tom &amp; Jerry &#8212; &lt;b&gt;")  # Tom & Jerry — <b>
```

- `url_encode = (s: str, safe: str = "") -> str`: text as a part of a URL (a query's value, a path's segment): every byte but letters, digits and - _ . ~ as %XX (a space as %20, é as %C3%A9), as Python's quote(s, safe="") writes it; the characters in `safe` are kept too: url_encode("/a b/c", safe = "/") is "/a%20b/c" (a path)
- `url_decode = (s: str, plus: bool = false) -> str`: percent-encoded text back as it was: %XX as that byte (a % without two hex digits stays as it is); with plus = true a + is a space too, as an HTML form's query writes one
- `url_query[T] = (params: {str: T}) -> str`: a query string from names and values, each url_encoded: url_query({"q": "x y", "n": "5"}) is "q=x%20y&n=5" (what goes after the ? of a URL; Python's urlencode)
- `parse_query = (q: str) -> {str: str}`: a query string's names and values, decoded: parse_query("q=x%20y&n=5") is {"q": "x y", "n": "5"} (what comes after the ? of a URL, or an HTML form's body; a name given twice: the last)
- `parse_cookies = (header: str) -> {str: str}`: a Cookie header's names and values: parse_cookies("a=1; b=2") is {"a": "1", "b": "2"}
- `struct Url`: a URL taken apart (parse_url), each part as written (its %XX kept: url_decode(u.path) for the text): https://ann@example.com:8080/a/b?q=1#top has scheme "https", user "ann", host "example.com", port 8080, path "/a/b", query "q=1" and fragment "top". The scheme and host are in small letters; an IPv6 host is without its [ ]. A part not written is "" (the port: -1). str(u) puts it back together.
