  ```gdscript
  struct Url:
      scheme = ""
      user = ""                   # (before an @: a name, or name:password)
      host = ""
      port = -1
      path = ""
      query = ""                  # (after the ?, without it: parse_query(u.query) for its values)
      fragment = ""               # (after the #, without it)
  ```
- `error struct UrlError`
  ```gdscript
  error struct UrlError:
      message: str
  ```
- `parse_url = (s: str) -> Url \| UrlError`: a URL's parts, or what is wrong with it (a port that is not a number or is over 65535, a [ without its ], a control character such as a line break in it). Spaces and line breaks around it are left out. A URL with no scheme is a relative one ("/a/b?q=1", "../c": scheme and host "")
  ```gdscript
  u = parse_url("https://example.com:8443/search?q=sloppy")?
  print(u.host, u.port, parse_query(u.query)["q"])      # example.com 8443 sloppy
  ```
- `url_join = (base: str, link: str) -> str`: a link (an href, a redirect's Location) as the URL it leads to from the page at base: "../c?x" from https://example.com/a/b/page.html is https://example.com/a/c?x. As RFC 3986 says (section 5.2: ./ and ../ taken out, a path from / on the same host, //host on the same scheme, a ?query or a #fragment on the same page), as Python's urljoin and Go's ResolveReference do. A link with its own scheme ("mailto:...", "https://...") is the link itself (with its ./ and ../ out); spaces and line breaks around it, and tabs and line breaks inside it (as in HTML written over lines), are left out.
  ```gdscript
  loop m in page.find_all(regex("href=\"([^\"]*)\"")): links.push(url_join(page_url, m.groups[0]))
  ```
- `html_unescape = (s: str) -> str`: HTML's entities as the characters they stand for: &amp; &lt; &gt; &quot; &#39; &nbsp;, the other named ones of Latin-1 and punctuation (&eacute; &copy; &mdash; &hellip; &euro;...), and numbers (&#233; &#xE9;); one it does not know stays as it is
- `html_escape = (s: str) -> str`: text with & < > " ' as entities, to put inside HTML

### base64.jo

Base64: bytes as text (A-Z a-z 0-9 + /, padded with =), and back.

- `base64_encode = (b: u8[]) -> str`
- `base64_decode = (s: str) -> u8[]?`: the bytes, or none if s is not base64 (spaces and line breaks in it are skipped)

### hash.jo

SHA-256: a file's or a text's fingerprint, as 64 hex digits (the same as `sha256sum` prints). Two different contents practically never have the same one: to check a download, or to know whether a file changed since it was last built.

- `sha256 = (b: u8[]) -> str`: the SHA-256 of these bytes, as 64 lowercase hex digits
- `sha256 = (s: str) -> str`: the SHA-256 of a text's UTF-8 bytes: sha256("abc") is ba7816bf...

## Bytes and bits

Whole numbers in byte arrays in either byte order (file formats, network messages), counting and turning bits, and bytes from C. A float's bits: `f64_bits` and `f64_from_bits` (math).

```gdscript
# example: bytes
# a WAV file's header, written and read back
n = 1000
h: u8[]
loop c in "RIFF": h.push(c)
append_le(h, 36 + n * 2, 4)
loop c in "WAVEfmt ": h.push(c)
append_le(h, 16, 4)
append_le(h, 1, 2)                       # PCM
append_le(h, 1, 2)                       # one channel
append_le(h, 44100, 4)
print(h.len(), get_le(h, 4, 4), get_le(h, 24, 4))
print(popcount(0b1011), leading_zeros(1), trailing_zeros(8), byte_swap(0x1234, 2) == 0x3412)
```

Bytes and bits: whole numbers in byte arrays (file formats, network messages) in little- or big-endian order, bit counting and turning, and bytes from C.

```gdscript
header: u8[]
append_le(header, 0x46464952, 4)              # "RIFF"
append_le(header, 36 + n, 4)
size = get_le(file, 4, 4)                     # 4 bytes at offset 4, little-endian
rate = get_be(packet, 0, 2)
rate_f = f32_from_bits(u32(get_le(file, 8, 4)))   # a float's bytes
```

Sizes are 1 to 8 bytes (3 for a 24-bit field); get_le and get_be give the bytes as an unsigned number (8 bytes: its bits as an int), get_le_signed and get_be_signed with their sign. A float's bits are a u64 (f64_bits) and these take an int: `append_le(b, int(f64_bits(x)), 8)`, and back `f64_from_bits(u64(get_le(b, at, 8)))`; to_bytes(xs) and array_from_bytes[f64](b) do a whole array.

- `get_le = (b: u8[], at: int, size: int) -> int`: the size-byte number at b[at..], least significant byte first
- `get_be = (b: u8[], at: int, size: int) -> int`: the size-byte number at b[at..], most significant byte first (network order)
- `get_le_signed = (b: u8[], at: int, size: int) -> int`
- `get_be_signed = (b: u8[], at: int, size: int) -> int`
- `put_le = (b: mut u8[], at: int, size: int, v: int)`: v as size bytes at b[at..] (b grows to hold them), least or most significant byte first
- `put_be = (b: mut u8[], at: int, size: int, v: int)`
- `append_le = (b: mut u8[], v: int, size: int)`: v as size bytes at the end of b
- `append_be = (b: mut u8[], v: int, size: int)`

#### Bits

- `popcount = (x: int) -> int`: how many bits are 1
- `leading_zeros = (x: int) -> int`: how many 0 bits come before the highest 1 (64 for 0)
- `trailing_zeros = (x: int) -> int`: how many 0 bits come after the lowest 1 (64 for 0)
- `rotate_left = (x: int, n: int) -> int`: the bits turned by n places (those going out at one end come back at the other)
- `rotate_right = (x: int, n: int) -> int`
- `popcount = (x: u8) -> int`: (each of the above for a number of each size, counting and turning its own bits: a u32's 32)
- `leading_zeros = (x: u8) -> int`
- `trailing_zeros = (x: u8) -> int`
- `rotate_left = (x: u8, n: int) -> u8`
- `rotate_right = (x: u8, n: int) -> u8`
- `popcount = (x: u16) -> int`
- `leading_zeros = (x: u16) -> int`
- `trailing_zeros = (x: u16) -> int`
- `rotate_left = (x: u16, n: int) -> u16`
- `rotate_right = (x: u16, n: int) -> u16`
- `popcount = (x: u32) -> int`
- `leading_zeros = (x: u32) -> int`
- `trailing_zeros = (x: u32) -> int`
- `rotate_left = (x: u32, n: int) -> u32`
- `rotate_right = (x: u32, n: int) -> u32`
- `popcount = (x: u64) -> int`
- `leading_zeros = (x: u64) -> int`
- `trailing_zeros = (x: u64) -> int`
- `rotate_left = (x: u64, n: int) -> u64`
- `rotate_right = (x: u64, n: int) -> u64`
- `popcount = (x: i8) -> int`
- `leading_zeros = (x: i8) -> int`
- `trailing_zeros = (x: i8) -> int`
- `rotate_left = (x: i8, n: int) -> i8`
- `rotate_right = (x: i8, n: int) -> i8`
- `popcount = (x: i16) -> int`
- `leading_zeros = (x: i16) -> int`
- `trailing_zeros = (x: i16) -> int`
- `rotate_left = (x: i16, n: int) -> i16`
- `rotate_right = (x: i16, n: int) -> i16`
- `popcount = (x: i32) -> int`
- `leading_zeros = (x: i32) -> int`
- `trailing_zeros = (x: i32) -> int`
- `rotate_left = (x: i32, n: int) -> i32`
- `rotate_right = (x: i32, n: int) -> i32`
- `byte_swap = (x: int, size: int = 8) -> int`: the bytes of a size-byte number in the other order (0x1234, 2 -> 0x3412)

#### Plain values

- `to_bytes[T] = (x: T) -> u8[]`: a plain value's bytes as it is in memory (numbers, bools, vectors, fixed-size arrays and structs of them; no str or T[]): a machine's state saved in one go, `write_bytes(path, to_bytes(cpu))`. They are this program's layout of the type, on a little-endian machine (every one Sloppy runs on): for reading back with from_bytes, not a file format for other programs
- `from_bytes[T] = (b: u8[]) -> T?`: the plain value whose bytes are b (from to_bytes): `from_bytes[Cpu](read_bytes(path) ?? [])`; none when b is not the size of a T
- `to_bytes[T] = (xs: T[]) -> u8[]`: an array of plain values: its elements' bytes one after another (n f64s are 8n bytes, as in a C array and NumPy's tobytes): `write_bytes(path, to_bytes(weights))`
- `array_from_bytes[T] = (b: u8[]) -> T[]?`: the array whose elements' bytes are b (from to_bytes): `array_from_bytes[f64](read_bytes(path) ?? [])`; none when b is not a whole number of them

#### From C

- `bytes_from = (p: *u8, n: int) -> u8[]`: a copy of the n bytes at p
- `str_from_c = (p: *u8) -> str`: a copy of C's text at p, up to its zero byte ("" for null)

## Saving, and values as text

`save_value` and `load_value` keep any value (settings, a saved game) as text that reads back even after its structs gain or lose fields.

```gdscript
# example: saving
# (a place of the program's own: a folder for the user on the desktop, the page's storage on the web)
struct Settings:
    volume = 0.8
    fullscreen = false
    keys: {str: int} = {"jump": 32}
    best_times: f64[]

settings = load_value("settings", Settings())      # (the defaults the first time)
settings.best_times.push(42.5)
save_value("settings", settings)
print(to_text(settings))

runs = to_int(load_data("runs") ?? "0") ?? 0        # (plain text works too)
save_data("runs", "{runs + 1}")
```

### save.jo

Data a program keeps between runs: saved games, settings, high scores.

```gdscript
save_data("progress", "{level}\n{coins}")
if let s = load_data("progress"): ...
```

Each program has its own place, named after it (PROGRAM_NAME: the build block's `name`, else the main file's name): on Linux ~/.local/share/<name>/ (or $XDG_DATA_HOME), on Windows %APPDATA%\<name>\, on macOS ~/Library/Application Support/<name>/, in a web page the browser's storage for the page (it survives reloads and restarts of the browser). SLOPPY_SAVE_DIR=/tmp/x puts it in /tmp/x/<name>/ instead (tests). For a file the player names (an editor's levels), write the text yourself: `write_file(path, to_text(level))` and `from_text[Level](read_file(path) ?? "")`.

- `save_dir = () -> str`: the directory a program's data goes to (made when first written; "" in a web page)
- `save_data = (name: str, contents: str) -> bool`: keep contents under name (a file name, as Windows allows them: no / \ : * ? " < > |); false if it could not be written (file_error() says why)
- `load_data = (name: str) -> str?`: what was kept under name; none if nothing was
- `delete_data = (name: str) -> bool`: forget what was kept under name (a saved game, at the player's death); false if nothing was

### text.jo

Values as text and back, for saved games and settings. to_text(value) writes a value the way print shows one inside a list (strings in quotes):

```gdscript
Settings(volume=0.8, keys={"jump": 32}, mode=hard, pos=vec2(1.5, 2.0), best=none)
```

from_text[T](text) reads such text back as a T, or says where it is not one (a TextError). Fields go by name, in any order: one the text leaves out keeps its default value, and one the struct does not have (any more) is skipped, so what an older version of a program wrote still reads after its structs change.

```gdscript
save_value("settings", settings)
settings = load_value("settings", Settings())     # (the default when there is none yet)
```

Numbers, bool, str, arrays (T[] and T[N]), maps, optionals, tuples, structs, enums (with fields too), unions and vectors can be read; function values and pointers cannot.

- `error struct TextError`
  ```gdscript
  error struct TextError:
      message: str
      line: int
      column: int
      path = ""       # where in the value: items[2].count (the message names it too)
  ```
- `to_text[T] = (x: T) -> str`
- `from_text[T] = (text: str) -> T \| TextError`
- `from_json[T] = (text: str) -> T \| TextError`: a value of type T from JSON: objects for structs (by field name: a field left out keeps its default, one the struct does not have is skipped) and maps, lists for arrays, tuples and vectors, null for none, an enum's variant by its name in quotes
- `save_value[T] = (name: str, x: T) -> bool`: keep value under name (save_data) as text; false if it could not be written
- `load_value[T] = (name: str, default: T) -> T`: the value kept under name, or default if there is none or it does not read as one (said on stderr then, and what was kept is copied to name + ".bad" first: the next save_value over it does not lose it)

## Pools and handles

`Pool[T]` holds things that come and go (enemies, bullets), each found again by its `Handle`, which stays its own while others are removed (an array's index does not) and says so once its thing is gone.

```gdscript
# example: pool
# (homing shots that keep their target: each enemy is found again by its handle, while others
# are shot down and removed; click to fire)
struct Enemy:
    pos: vec2
    hp = 3
struct Shot:
    pos: vec2
    target: Handle
enemies: Pool[Enemy]
shots: Shot[]
loop i in 0..6: enemies.add(Enemy(vec2(f32(200 + i * 160), 120)))

update = (dt: f64):
    if mouse_pressed(.left) and enemies.len() > 0:
        shots.push(Shot(vec2(640, 680), enemies.handles[random_int(0, enemies.len())]))
    loop mut s in shots:
        if not enemies.has(s.target): continue
        to = enemies[s.target].pos - s.pos
        if length(to) < 12.0:
            enemies[s.target].hp -= 1
            s.target = Handle()
        else: s.pos += normalize(to) * f32(500.0 * dt)
    shots.retain((s): enemies.has(s.target))
    enemies.retain((e): e.hp > 0)

draw = ():
    clear()
    loop e in enemies.items: circle(e.pos, 10.0 + f64(e.hp) * 4.0, RED)
    loop s in shots: circle(s.pos, 4, YELLOW)
```

Things that come and go (enemies, bullets, units), each found again by a Handle: an index into an array points at another thing once things before it are removed, a handle stays its own, and says so once its thing is gone. The things themselves are an ordinary array, `items`, in no particular order, to loop over as any.

```gdscript
enemies: Pool[Enemy]
h = enemies.add(Enemy(pos))
bullet.target = h                     # (a homing bullet keeps its enemy)
if enemies.has(bullet.target): aim = enemies[bullet.target].pos
enemies[h].hp -= 10
loop mut e in enemies.items: e.pos += e.vel * dt
enemies.retain((e): e.hp > 0)         # (handles to those gone: has(h) is false)
```

- `struct Handle`: a thing's handle in a Pool (Handle() is none: has(h) false)
  ```gdscript
  struct Handle:
      slot = -1
      gen = 0
  ```
- `struct Pool[T]`
  ```gdscript
  struct Pool[T]:
      items: T[]                  # the things, in no particular order
      handles: Handle[]           # items[i]'s handle
  ```
- `add[T] = (p: mut Pool[T], x: T) -> Handle`: add x: its handle
- `has[T] = (p: Pool[T], h: Handle) -> bool`: whether h's thing is still there
- `index_of[T] = (p: Pool[T], h: Handle) -> int`: the index of h's thing in items now (-1 when it is gone)
- `([])[T] = (p: Pool[T], h: Handle) -> T`: h's thing (panics when it is gone: has(h) first, or get)
- `([]=)[T] = (p: mut Pool[T], h: Handle, x: T)`
- `get[T] = (p: Pool[T], h: Handle) -> T?`: h's thing, or none when it is gone
- `remove[T] = (p: mut Pool[T], h: Handle) -> bool`: take h's thing out (false when it was gone already); the last thing moves into its place in items
- `retain[T] = (p: mut Pool[T], keep: (T) -> bool)`: keep the things keep says yes to; the others' handles are gone
- `len[T] = (p: Pool[T]) -> int`
- `clear[T] = (p: mut Pool[T])`: everything out (every handle gone)

## Sets and deques

`Set[T]` holds values without repeats (`x in s` asks in about the same time however many it holds); `Deque[T]` is a queue with two ends, pushed and popped at either in the same short time: a breadth-first search's frontier.

```gdscript
# example: sets
# the rooms one can walk to from the hall, nearest first: a breadth-first search with a Deque
# as its frontier and a Set of the rooms seen
doors = {"hall": ["kitchen", "stairs"], "kitchen": ["pantry", "hall"], "stairs": ["attic", "hall"],
    "pantry": [], "attic": ["roof"], "roof": []}
seen = set(["hall"])
frontier: Deque[(str, int)]
frontier.push(("hall", 0))
loop frontier.len() > 0:
    room, steps = frontier.pop_front()
    print("{room}: {steps} doors away")
    loop next in doors[room]:
        if seen.add(next): frontier.push((next, steps + 1))
upstairs = set(["stairs", "attic", "roof"])
print(seen.difference(upstairs), "roof" in seen, seen.len())
```

### set.jo

A set: values without repeats, kept in the order they were first added, asked about in about the same time however many it holds (a map from each to nothing, underneath).

```gdscript
seen: Set[int]                      # (or seen: Set[int] = {}, empty)
seen.add(start)
vowels = {"a", "e", "i", "o", "u"}  # the values written; {loop w in ws: w.lower()} too
if next in seen: continue           # x in s, or s.has(x)
words = set(split(text, " "))       # the different words
loop w in words: print(w)
both = a.intersection(b)            # also union, difference, is_subset
```

Sets compare with `==` by what they hold, in any order, and print as {1, 2, 3}.

- `struct Set[T]`
- `set[T] = (xs: T[]) -> Set[T]`: a set of the values in xs (each once)
- `add[T] = (s: mut Set[T], x: T) -> bool`: put x in; true when it was not there yet (`if seen.add(v): queue.push(v)`)
- `remove[T] = (s: mut Set[T], x: T) -> bool`: take x out; true when it was there
- `has[T] = (s: Set[T], x: T) -> bool`
- `contains[T] = (s: Set[T], x: T) -> bool`: (x in s)
- `len[T] = (s: Set[T]) -> int`
- `is_empty[T] = (s: Set[T]) -> bool`
- `clear[T] = (s: mut Set[T])`
- `items[T] = (s: Set[T]) -> T[]`: the values, in the order they were added (a loop over the set goes over these)
- `union[T] = (a: Set[T], b: Set[T]) -> Set[T]`: what is in a, in b, or in both
- `intersection[T] = (a: Set[T], b: Set[T]) -> Set[T]`: what is in both a and b
- `difference[T] = (a: Set[T], b: Set[T]) -> Set[T]`: what is in a and not in b
- `is_subset[T] = (a: Set[T], b: Set[T]) -> bool`: whether everything in a is in b
- `(==)[T] = (a: Set[T], b: Set[T]) -> bool`: the same values, in any order
- `hash[T] = (s: Set[T]) -> u64`: a number made from the values, in any order: sets that are == hash alike (so a set can be a key)

### deque.jo

A double-ended queue: values pushed and popped at either end in the same short time, however many it holds (an array's insert(0, x) and remove(0) move all the others). A breadth-first search's frontier, a sliding window, an undo list with a limit.

```gdscript
q: Deque[int]
q.push(start)                       # at the back
loop q.len() > 0:
    v = q.pop_front()               # the oldest
    ...q.push(next)
q.push_front(x)                     # at the front; q.pop() takes from the back
q.first(), q.last(), q[i]           # (i from the front)
```

A loop over a deque goes from front to back; it prints as an array does.

- `struct Deque[T]`
- `len[T] = (d: Deque[T]) -> int`
- `is_empty[T] = (d: Deque[T]) -> bool`
- `push[T] = (d: mut Deque[T], x: T)`: put x at the back
- `push_front[T] = (d: mut Deque[T], x: T)`: put x at the front
- `pop[T] = (d: mut Deque[T]) -> T`: take the value at the back out (the deque must not be empty)
- `pop_front[T] = (d: mut Deque[T]) -> T`: take the value at the front out (the deque must not be empty)
- `first[T] = (d: Deque[T]) -> T`: the value at the front, and at the back (the deque must not be empty)
- `last[T] = (d: Deque[T]) -> T`
- `([])[T] = (d: Deque[T], i: int) -> T`: the i-th value from the front
- `([]=)[T] = (d: mut Deque[T], i: int, x: T)`
- `clear[T] = (d: mut Deque[T])`
- `items[T] = (d: Deque[T]) -> T[]`: the values from front to back, in an array (a loop over the deque goes over these)
- `(==)[T] = (a: Deque[T], b: Deque[T]) -> bool`: the same values in the same order
- `hash[T] = (d: Deque[T]) -> u64`: a number made from the values, in order: deques that are == hash alike (so a deque can be a key)

## Priority queues

`Heap[T]` gives back what it holds smallest priority first: A*'s open list, events by time. `heap_push` and `heap_pop` keep a plain array as a heap of values that order themselves, as Python's heapq does.

```gdscript
# example: heap
# the shortest way across a small map (Dijkstra): the open list is a Heap, cheapest first
map = ["S..#....", ".#.#.##.", ".#...#..", ".####.#.", "......#E"]
w = len(map[0])
cost: {int: f64} = {}
open: Heap[int]
open.push(0, 0.0)
cost[0] = 0.0
loop open.len() > 0:
    c = open.pop()
    x = c % w
    y = c / w
    if map[y][x] == 'E':
        print("E reached in {cost[c]} steps")
        break
    loop d in [(1, 0), (-1, 0), (0, 1), (0, -1)]:
        nx = x + d.0
        ny = y + d.1
        if nx < 0 or ny < 0 or nx >= w or ny >= map.len() or map[ny][nx] == '#': continue
        n = ny * w + nx
        if cost.has(n) and cost[n] <= cost[c] + 1.0: continue
        cost[n] = cost[c] + 1.0
        open.push(n, cost[n])
```

A priority queue: values taken out smallest priority first, as A*'s open list or events by time. Pushing and popping take time in proportion to the log of how many it holds.

```gdscript
open: Heap[int]
open.push(start, 0.0)
loop open.len() > 0:
    c, d = open.pop_with_priority()    # the one with the least priority, and that priority
    ...open.push(next, d + cost)
```

Priorities are f64s, or ints: an int priority keeps its order exactly, past 2^53 too, where f64s of neighboring ints are equal (pop_with_int_priority gives one back exactly). (largest first: push with -priority.) Values that order themselves, as Python's heapq keeps them, need no Heap: `pq.heap_push((dist, node))` and `pq.heap_pop()` on an array keep the smallest at pq[0], tuples compared by their first element, then the next.

- `struct Heap[T]`
  ```gdscript
  struct Heap[T]:
      items: T[]
      keys: f64[]
  ```
- `push[T] = (h: mut Heap[T], x: T, priority: f64)`: put x in with this priority
- `push[T] = (h: mut Heap[T], x: T, priority: int)`: put x in with an int priority, kept exactly (an int past 2^53 is not, as an f64)
- `pop[T] = (h: mut Heap[T]) -> T`: take out the value with the least priority (the heap must not be empty)
- `pop_with_priority[T] = (h: mut Heap[T]) -> (T, f64)`: take out the value with the least priority, and give that priority too (the heap must not be empty)
- `pop_with_int_priority[T] = (h: mut Heap[T]) -> (T, int)`: take out the value with the least priority, and give that priority as an int, exactly as pushed with an int (the heap must not be empty)
- `peek[T] = (h: Heap[T]) -> T`: the value pop would take out (the heap must not be empty)
- `peek_priority[T] = (h: Heap[T]) -> f64`: the least priority, the one of the value pop would take out (the heap must not be empty)
- `peek_int_priority[T] = (h: Heap[T]) -> int`: the least priority as an int, exactly as pushed with an int (the heap must not be empty)
- `len[T] = (h: Heap[T]) -> int`
- `clear[T] = (h: mut Heap[T])`

#### An array as a heap

- `heap_push[T] = (xs: mut T[], x: T)`: put x in the array kept as a heap
- `heapify[T] = (xs: mut T[])`: the array made a heap in place, in time in proportion to its length (Python's heapify): heap_pop then takes out its smallest
- `heap_pop[T] = (xs: mut T[]) -> T`: take the smallest value out of the array kept as a heap (it must not be empty)

## Stories and dialogue

A story's script, written as Ren'Py writes one (labels, lines, menus, variables), read into a `Story` the game steps through: it draws each line and choice its own way (a dialogue box, `text_rich`) and does each command.

```gdscript
# example: story
# a story's script, stepped through: a game draws each line and choice its own way
script = r"""
character m "Mira" #f4a6c6

label start:
    scene cafe
    m "You came!"
    menu:
        "Of course.":
            $ trust += 1
        "I almost didn't.":
            $ trust -= 1
    if trust > 0:
        m "Then stay a while."
    else:
        m "Oh."
"""
st = story(script)
loop true:
    step = st.next()
    if step.kind == .end: break
    if step.kind == .line: print("{step.speaker}: {step.text}")
    else if step.kind == .command: print("(the game does: {join(step.words, " ")})")
    else:
        print("choices: {step.choices}")
        st.choose(0)
print(st.vars)
```

Stories: a script of dialogue, choices and what happens on screen, written as Ren'Py writes one, read into a Story that the game steps through. The game draws each line and choice its own way (a dialogue box, text_rich, portraits) and does each command (show, scene, play...).

```gdscript
character m "Mira" #f4a6c6

label start:
    scene cafe
    show mira happy
    "The bell rang twice."                 (narration)
    m "You came! I wasn't sure you would."
    menu:
        "Of course I came.":
            $ mira += 2
        "I almost didn't." if mira < 5:
            $ mira -= 1
            jump doubt
    if mira >= 3:
        m "Then stay a while."
    else:
        jump goodbye

st = story(str_from_bytes(embed("story.txt")))
if len(st.error) > 0: print(st.error)
step = st.next()            # (again when the player clicks: the next line, choice or command)
match step.kind:
    .line: draw step.speaker and step.text (step.who: the character's id, for a portrait)
    .choice: draw step.choices; on a click, st.choose(i) and step = st.next()
    .command: do step.words ("show", "mira", "happy"), then step = st.next()
    .end: the end
```

Lines: `label name:` (the story starts at `start`, else at the top), `character id "Name" #color`, "narration", `id "dialogue"`, `menu:` with "options": (and `if` conditions), `if`, `elif`, `else`, `jump label`, `call label` and `return`, `$ name = value` (and += -=), `end`. Anything else is a command for the game. Variables are whole numbers (true is 1, false 0), and "[name]" in a line puts one in. A Story is a value: save_value("story", st.save()) and later st.load(load_value("story", st.save())) take up where it was, even after the script changed in other labels.

- `enum StoryKind: line, choice, command, end`
- `struct StoryStep`: what the story does next
  ```gdscript
  struct StoryStep:
      kind: StoryKind
      speaker = ""        # a line's speaker ("": narration)
      who = ""            # the speaker's id, as in the script (m)
      color = ""          # the speaker's color, as written (#f4a6c6; "": none given)
      text = ""           # a line's text, with [name]s put in
      choices: str[]      # a choice's options (those whose condition holds)
      words: str[]        # a command's words
  ```
- `struct Story`: a story read from its script; error says what is wrong with it ("" if nothing)
  ```gdscript
  struct Story:
      error = ""
      vars: {str: int}
  ```
- `struct StorySave`: where a story is, to save and load: the place by label and statement, so it still loads after the script changes elsewhere
  ```gdscript
  struct StorySave:
      label = ""
      nth = 0
      stack: (str, int)[]
      vars: {str: int}
  ```
- `story = (text: str) -> Story`: a story from its script (st.error: what is wrong with it, with its line)
- `next = (st: mut Story) -> StoryStep`: what the story does next: a line to show, a choice to make (until choose), a command for the game, or the end
- `choose = (st: mut Story, i: int)`: take option i of the choice next gave (from 0); the story goes on from there
- `jump = (st: mut Story, label: str) -> bool`: go to a label (a chapter select, a new game)
- `save = (st: Story) -> StorySave`: where the story is (for save_value), and back there (a StorySave from an older script finds its label's statement)
- `load = (st: mut Story, s: StorySave)`

## Charts

Line charts, scatter plots, bars and histograms with axes and a legend: drawn in a game's `draw`, or made into a picture with no window (`chart_image`, then `save_png`). `render_image` draws any picture that way.

```gdscript
# example: charts
# a line chart and a histogram, made into pictures (save_png(img, "sales.png") keeps one)
r = rng(1)
days = [loop i in 0..60: f64(i)]
sales = [loop d in days: 100.0 + 2.0 * d + 10.0 * r.next_normal()]
c = Chart(title = "Sales", x_label = "day", y_label = "units")
c.line(days, sales, "units sold")
c.line([0.0, 59.0], [100.0, 218.0], "trend")
img = chart_image(c, 640, 400)
print(img.width, img.height)

h = Chart(title = "How sales spread")
h.histogram(sales, 15)
print(chart_image(h, 400, 300).width)
```

Charts: lines, points, bars and histograms over axes with ticks, labels and a legend. A Chart holds what to show; draw_chart draws it into a rectangle (in a game's draw, a live graph), and chart_image makes a picture of it with no window (a script's chart, saved with save_png).

```gdscript
c = Chart(title = "Daily sales", x_label = "day", y_label = "revenue")
c.line(days, revenue, "revenue")
c.line(days, weekly_mean, "7-day mean")
c.points(xs, ys, "orders")
save_png(chart_image(c, 800, 500), "sales.png")

c2 = Chart(title = "Units by region")
c2.bars(["north", "south", "east"], [120.0, 95.0, 143.0], "2026")
c3 = Chart(title = "Prices")
c3.histogram(prices, 30)
```

Each series given no color takes the next of a palette. The axes fit what is shown (from 0 for bars and histograms) unless x_min, x_max, y_min or y_max say otherwise; equal_axes keeps a unit as long across as up (a trajectory's true shape). Whole numbers and f32s (a vec2's parts) may be given as well as f64s. c.annotate(x, y, "apex") writes a word at a point.

- `struct Chart`
  ```gdscript
  struct Chart:
      title = ""
      x_label = ""
      y_label = ""
      series: ChartSeries[]
      categories: str[]           # (bars: the labels along the x axis)
      x_min: f64? = none
      x_max: f64? = none
      y_min: f64? = none
      y_max: f64? = none
      legend = true
      legend_at = LegendAt.top_right      # (the corner of the plot it is in)
      grid = true
      equal_axes = false                  # a unit as long along x as along y
      background = vec4(1, 1, 1, 1)
      ink = vec4(0.15, 0.15, 0.17, 1)     # text and axes
      text_size = 14.0
      log_y = false                       # y on a log scale: each tick ten times the one below
  ```
- `enum LegendAt: top_right, top_left, bottom_right, bottom_left`
- `struct ChartSeries`
  ```gdscript
  struct ChartSeries:
      kind: int                   # 0 line, 1 points, 2 bars, 3 histogram, 4 a note (its name)
      xs: f64[]
      ys: f64[]
      name: str
      color: vec4
      size: f64                   # a line's width, a point's radius
      bin_width = 0.0             # (a histogram's)
      dash = 0.0                  # (a line's dashes' length, in pixels; 0: solid)
  ```
- `line = (c: mut Chart, xs: f64[], ys: f64[], name: str = "", color: vec4? = none, width: f64 = 2.0, dash: f64 = 0.0)`: a line through the points (x, y), in order; dash: dashes this many pixels long (0: solid)
- `line = (c: mut Chart, xs: f32[], ys: f32[], name: str = "", color: vec4? = none, width: f64 = 2.0, dash: f64 = 0.0)`
- `line = (c: mut Chart, xs: int[], ys: int[], name: str = "", color: vec4? = none, width: f64 = 2.0, dash: f64 = 0.0)`
- `line = (c: mut Chart, ys: f64[], name: str = "", color: vec4? = none, width: f64 = 2.0, dash: f64 = 0.0)`: a line through ys, at x = 0, 1, 2...
- `line = (c: mut Chart, ys: f32[], name: str = "", color: vec4? = none, width: f64 = 2.0, dash: f64 = 0.0)`
- `line = (c: mut Chart, ys: int[], name: str = "", color: vec4? = none, width: f64 = 2.0, dash: f64 = 0.0)`
- `points = (c: mut Chart, xs: f64[], ys: f64[], name: str = "", color: vec4? = none, radius: f64 = 3.0)`: a dot at each point (a scatter plot)
- `points = (c: mut Chart, xs: f32[], ys: f32[], name: str = "", color: vec4? = none, radius: f64 = 3.0)`
- `points = (c: mut Chart, xs: int[], ys: int[], name: str = "", color: vec4? = none, radius: f64 = 3.0)`
- `bars = (c: mut Chart, labels: str[], values: f64[], name: str = "", color: vec4? = none)`: a bar for each category (more bars series stand side by side)
- `bars = (c: mut Chart, labels: str[], values: int[], name: str = "", color: vec4? = none)`
- `bars = (c: mut Chart, labels: str[], values: f32[], name: str = "", color: vec4? = none)`
- `annotate = (c: mut Chart, x: f64, y: f64, words: str, color: vec4? = none)`: words written at the point (x, y), in the ink's color unless given one (not in the legend)
- `histogram = (c: mut Chart, values: f64[], bins: int = 20, name: str = "", color: vec4? = none)`: how the values spread: how many fall in each of `bins` equal ranges (NaN left out)
- `chart_image = (c: Chart, w: int, h: int) -> Image`: a picture of the chart, w x h pixels (no window needed)
- `draw_chart = (c: Chart, r: Rect)`: draw the chart in a rectangle (its background, axes, series, title and legend)

## Cryptography

A module (`import crypto`): SHA-1, SHA-256, SHA-384 and SHA-512, HMAC, HKDF, TLS 1.2's PRF, AES-128-GCM, P-256 and X25519 keys (ECDH), P-256 signatures (ECDSA), P-384 and RSA signatures checked, self-signed certificates and random bytes from the operating system; checked against the standards' test vectors and Wycheproof's.

Cryptography: hashes (SHA-1, SHA-256, SHA-384, SHA-512), HMAC, HKDF, TLS 1.2's PRF, AES-128-GCM, the P-256 curve (ECDH keys and ECDSA signatures), X25519 keys, P-384 and RSA signatures checked, a self-signed certificate, and random bytes from the operating system. It is what the library's WebRTC (lib/rtc.jo) and TLS (lib/tls.jo) are made of, and a program may use it too: `import crypto`.

```gdscript
key = crypto.random_bytes(16)
sealed = crypto.aes_gcm_seal(key, nonce12, aad, message)        # the message and a 16-byte tag
opened = crypto.aes_gcm_open(key, nonce12, aad, sealed)         # none when it was changed
pk = crypto.p256_key()                                          # a private key and its public point
sig = crypto.p256_sign(pk, crypto.sha256(msg))
ok = crypto.p256_verify(pk.public, crypto.sha256(msg), sig)
```

(P-256's arithmetic does not take the same time whatever the key: fine for keys made for a session, as WebRTC's are; not for long-lived keys on shared machines. X25519's does.)

#### Random bytes

- `random_bytes = (n: int) -> u8[]`: n bytes from the operating system's random source (for keys and nonces: not seeded, not repeatable)

#### Bytes

- `hex_text = (b: u8[]) -> str`: bytes as lowercase hex ("a9993e...")
- `hex_bytes = (s: str) -> u8[]?`: "a9993e..." as bytes (none when it is not hex)

#### MD5

- `md5 = (m: u8[]) -> u8[]`

#### SHA-1

- `sha1 = (m: u8[]) -> u8[]`: SHA-1 of the bytes (20 bytes); for HMAC-SHA1, which STUN uses, not for anything new

#### SHA-256

- `sha256 = (m: u8[]) -> u8[]`: SHA-256 of the bytes (32 bytes; std/hash's sha256 gives them as hex)

#### SHA-512, SHA-384

- `sha512 = (m: u8[]) -> u8[]`: SHA-512 of the bytes (64 bytes)
- `sha384 = (m: u8[]) -> u8[]`: SHA-384 of the bytes (48 bytes: SHA-512 started elsewhere, cut short)

#### HMAC

- `hmac_sha256 = (key: u8[], msg: u8[]) -> u8[]`
- `hmac_sha1 = (key: u8[], msg: u8[]) -> u8[]`

#### HKDF (RFC 5869, with SHA-256)

- `hkdf_extract = (salt: u8[], ikm: u8[]) -> u8[]`: a key of 32 bytes made from input keying material and a salt (empty: as 32 zeros)
- `hkdf_expand = (prk: u8[], info: u8[], n: int) -> u8[]`: n bytes of keys made from such a key, for a purpose (info)

#### TLS 1.2's PRF (RFC 5246 5, P_SHA256)

- `tls12_prf = (secret: u8[], label: str, seed: u8[], n: int) -> u8[]`: n bytes of keys made from a secret, for a purpose (label) and a seed

#### AES-128

- `struct AesKey`: an AES-128 key, expanded: the 11 round keys, 176 bytes (and as 44 big-endian words)
  ```gdscript
  struct AesKey:
      w: u8[176]
      rk: u32[44]
  ```
- `aes_key = (key: u8[]) -> AesKey`
- `aes_encrypt_block = (k: AesKey, block: u8[16]) -> u8[16]`: one 16-byte block encrypted

#### GHASH, constant-time

- `aes_gcm_seal = (key: AesKey, nonce: u8[], aad: u8[], message: u8[]) -> u8[]`: the message encrypted, then its 16-byte tag: nonce (12 bytes) must never repeat for one key; aad is bytes the tag covers that travel unencrypted
- `aes_gcm_open = (key: AesKey, nonce: u8[], aad: u8[], sealed: u8[]) -> u8[]?`: the message back from aes_gcm_seal's bytes, or none when they (or aad) were changed

#### P-256

- `struct P256Key`: a P-256 key: the private scalar and the public point (65 bytes: 0x04, x, y)
  ```gdscript
  struct P256Key:
      private: u8[]
      public: u8[]
  ```
- `p256_key = () -> P256Key`: a new key, from the system's random bytes
- `p256_ecdh = (k: P256Key, other: u8[]) -> u8[]?`: the shared secret of a key and another's public point (its x, 32 bytes), or none when that is no point on the curve
- `p256_sign = (k: P256Key, hash: u8[]) -> u8[]`: an ECDSA signature of a 32-byte hash: r and s, 32 bytes each
- `p256_verify = (public: u8[], hash: u8[], sig: u8[]) -> bool`: whether sig (r and s, 32 bytes each) signs the 32-byte hash for the public point

#### X25519 (RFC 7748)

- `struct X25519Key`: an X25519 key: the private scalar and the public value, 32 bytes each
  ```gdscript
  struct X25519Key:
      private: u8[]
      public: u8[]
  ```
- `x25519_key = () -> X25519Key`: a new key, from the system's random bytes
- `x25519_ecdh = (k: X25519Key, other: u8[]) -> u8[]?`: the shared secret of a key and another's public value (32 bytes), or none when it comes out all zeros (the other's value was one of the few that make it so)

#### RSA signatures (checking only)

- `rsa_verify_pkcs1 = (n: u8[], e: u8[], hash: u8[], sig: u8[]) -> bool`: whether sig is an RSASSA-PKCS1-v1_5 signature of the hash (as certificates are signed)
- `rsa_verify_pss = (n: u8[], e: u8[], hash: u8[], sig: u8[]) -> bool`: whether sig is an RSASSA-PSS signature of the hash, with MGF1 of the same hash and a salt as long as the hash (as TLS 1.3 signs)

#### P-384 (ECDSA signatures, checking only)

- `p384_verify = (public: u8[], hash: u8[], sig: u8[]) -> bool`: whether sig (r and s, 48 bytes each) signs the hash for the P-384 public point (97 bytes: 0x04, x, y); a hash longer than 48 bytes is cut to its first 48, as ECDSA does

#### DER

- `der_signature = (sig: u8[]) -> u8[]`: r and s (32 bytes each) as an ECDSA signature in DER (as TLS and certificates carry it)
- `der_signature_parts = (der: u8[]) -> u8[]?`: a DER ECDSA signature as r and s (32 bytes each), or none when it is not one
- `self_signed_certificate = (k: P256Key, name: str) -> u8[]`: a self-signed X.509 certificate for the key (DER), named name: what DTLS (WebRTC's) shows the other side, which knows it by its SHA-256 fingerprint
- `certificate_p256_key = (cert: u8[]) -> u8[]?`: the P-256 public point (65 bytes) a certificate holds, or none when it holds none

#### CRC-32

- `crc32 = (b: u8[]) -> u32`: CRC-32 (zip's, PNG's, STUN's) of the bytes
- `crc32c = (b: u8[]) -> u32`: CRC-32C (Castagnoli: SCTP's, iSCSI's) of the bytes

## Big numbers

A module (`use big`): numbers past f64's 1.8e308, a mantissa and a power of ten (an idle game's currency), with arithmetic, comparison, `min`, `max`, sorting and saving as for numbers; shown as 1.23M or 4.56e+1234.

Big numbers: past f64's 1.8e308, as an idle game's currency grows (1e1000, 1e100000). A Big is a mantissa from 1 to 10 and a power of ten, so it keeps about 15 digits, as a float does, whatever its size (BigInt, with no import, keeps every digit of a whole number); arithmetic, comparison, min, max and sort work as for numbers:

```gdscript
use big
dust = big(0)
rate = big(1.5, 120)                  # 1.5e120
dust += rate * dt
if dust >= cost: ...
print(dust)                           # 4.20e+1234 (short_number's form below 1e15)
```

to_text, to_json and saves write its fields (Big(m=4.2, e=1234)) and read them back.

- `struct Big`
  ```gdscript
  struct Big:
      m: f64          # 0, or from 1 up to (not including) 10 (negative: from -10)
      e: int          # the power of ten
  ```
- `big = (x: f64) -> Big`: x as a Big
- `big = (x: int) -> Big`
- `big = (m: f64, e: int) -> Big`: m times ten to the power e: big(1.5, 120) is 1.5e120
- `to_f64 = (b: Big) -> f64`: the f64 nearest a Big (infinity past 1.8e308, 0 below 1e-308)
- `(+) = (a: Big, b: Big) -> Big`
- `(-) = (a: Big, b: Big) -> Big`
- `(-) = (a: Big) -> Big`
- `(*) = (a: Big, b: Big) -> Big`
- `(/) = (a: Big, b: Big) -> Big`
- `(+) = (a: Big, x: f64) -> Big`
- `(-) = (a: Big, x: f64) -> Big`
- `(*) = (a: Big, x: f64) -> Big`
- `(/) = (a: Big, x: f64) -> Big`
- `(*) = (x: f64, a: Big) -> Big`
- `(<) = (a: Big, b: Big) -> bool`
- `(==) = (a: Big, b: Big) -> bool`
- `hash = (b: Big) -> u64`: a number made from b: the same for Bigs that are == (every zero alike), so a Big can be a key
- `(<) = (a: Big, x: f64) -> bool`
- `(<) = (x: f64, a: Big) -> bool`
- `log10 = (b: Big) -> f64`: the power of ten it is near: log10(big(1, 500)) is 500
- `pow = (b: Big, p: f64) -> Big`: b to the power p (p may be a fraction: a cost that grows 1.15 times for each one bought)
- `floor = (b: Big) -> Big`
- `abs = (b: Big) -> Big`
- `str = (b: Big) -> str`: how it shows: short_number's form below 1e15 (1.23K, 45.6M), else 1.23e+45

## Tasks and channels

`spawn((): ...)` runs a function alongside the rest of the program; a task that waits (a channel, the network, `sleep`, a line of input) lets the others run meanwhile. Channels carry values between them. Nothing is imported (the compiler reads it for the programs that use it). See [tasks](LANGUAGE.md#tasks-and-channels).

```gdscript
# example: tasks
# three tasks at once: each sleeps (the others run meanwhile), then sends what it found
found = channel[str]()
loop name in ["ann", "bob", "cy"]:
    spawn(():
        sleep(0.1)
        found.send("{name} is done")
    )
loop _ in 0..3: print(found.receive()!)

# a task's result
total = spawn((): [loop i in 0..1000: i].sum())
print(total.wait())
```

Tasks: functions that run alongside the rest of the program, taking turns with it, and channels that carry values from one to another. A task that waits (for a channel, a socket, a fetch, sleep, a line of input) lets the others run meanwhile:

```gdscript
t = spawn((): net.fetch("https://example.com").text)     # starts it; the program goes on
...
page = t.wait()                                      # its result (waiting for it if need be)

jobs = channel[str]()
results = channel[int]()
loop _ in 0..4:
    spawn(():
        loop let url = jobs.receive(): results.send(net.fetch(url).status)
    )
loop u in urls: jobs.send(u)
jobs.close()                     # the workers' loops end once they have taken every one
loop _ in urls: print(results.receive()!)
```

Tasks take turns on the program's one thread: one runs until it waits, then another. So between two waits nothing else changes the program's variables, and they need no locks; a task that computes for a long time without waiting holds up the others (parallel_map shares work over all of the processor's cores). A channel's send or receive that need not wait goes on at once, letting the others ready take their turns only every 64th time; run_program, file reads and writes, and a C function hold up every task while they run. The program ends when main and every task have ended, or at exit(); a game's tasks take their turns at the start of each frame, and end with it.

t.cancel() asks a task to stop: from then on its waits end at once, giving nothing (receive none, sleep returns, the network's calls nothing or status 0), and cancelled() is true in it, for it to clean up and end. A group (task_group) is tasks waited for and cancelled together.

#### Tasks

- `struct Task[T]`
- `spawn[T] = (f: () -> T, background: bool = false) -> Task[T]`: run f alongside the rest of the program; its result is the Task's wait(). background: the program's end does not wait for it (a server's loop, a task that watches something)
- `wait[T] = (t: Task[T]) -> T`: its result, once it has ended (until then the caller waits, and the others take their turns; also in a task that has been cancelled, as the result is needed: wait(wait = ...) is a wait that cancel ends)
- `wait[T] = (t: Task[T], wait: f64) -> bool`: wait at most `wait` seconds for it to end: true once it has (wait() then gives its result at once), false if it has not (or the task waiting has been cancelled)
  ```gdscript
  if not t.wait(wait = 5.0): t.cancel()
  ```
- `done[T] = (t: Task[T]) -> bool`: it has ended
- `wait_all[T] = (ts: Task[T][]) -> T[]`: the results of all of them, in their order
- `cancel[T] = (t: Task[T])`: ask it to stop: its waits end at once from now on, the one it is in too, giving nothing (a channel's receive none, a send gives up, sleep returns, select takes its after or else arm, the network's calls give nothing or status 0), and cancelled() is true in it, for it to clean up and end. Nothing if it has ended. A task that never waits or looks is not stopped
- `cancelled = () -> bool`: true in a task that has been cancelled (t.cancel(), or its group's): time for it to end
- `task_count = () -> int`: how many tasks have started and not ended (main not counted, those in the background counted)
- `report_tasks = ()`: write to standard error what each task is doing (what it waits for, where, and where it was spawned): for a program that seems stuck

#### Groups

- `struct TaskGroup`
- `task_group = () -> TaskGroup`: a group, with no tasks yet
- `spawn[T] = (g: TaskGroup, f: () -> T) -> Task[T]`: run f alongside the rest of the program, in group g (cancelled at once if g has been)
- `wait = (g: TaskGroup) -> bool`: wait until every task of the group has ended; false if one of them panicked (why() says what; the others were cancelled then). In a task that has been cancelled, the group's are cancelled first
- `cancel = (g: TaskGroup)`: cancel every task of the group (t.cancel()), and those spawned into it from now on
- `why = (g: TaskGroup) -> str`: the message of the first panic in the group's tasks ("": none has panicked)

#### Channels

- `struct Channel[T]`: Values sent from one task to another, in order. send waits until a receiver takes the value (capacity 0), or while capacity values wait to be taken; receive waits until one comes, and gives none once the channel is closed and every value has been taken. Copies of a Channel are the same channel.
- `channel[T] = (capacity: int = 0) -> Channel[T]`: a channel of T: channel[str](), or channel[int](100) for one that holds up to 100 values before send waits
- `send[T] = (c: Channel[T], v: T)`: send v: it waits until a receiver takes it (or while the channel is full). In a task that has been cancelled it waits for nothing: v is sent if there is room, else dropped (or left in the channel, when the send was waiting already)
- `send[T] = (c: Channel[T], v: T, wait: f64) -> bool`: send v if it can go within `wait` seconds (a receiver takes it, or there is room); false: it was not sent
- `try_send[T] = (c: Channel[T], v: T) -> bool`: send v if that needs no wait (a receiver waits for it, or there is room); false: not sent. When it is full and other tasks are ready (a receiver just woken), they take their turns first
- `receive[T] = (c: Channel[T]) -> T?`: the next value; none once the channel is closed and every value sent has been taken (or the task has been cancelled)
- `receive[T] = (c: Channel[T], wait: f64) -> T?`: the next value, waiting at most `wait` seconds for it; none when none came (or the channel is closed and empty: closed() tells which)
- `try_receive[T] = (c: Channel[T]) -> T?`: the next value if one waits; none if not
- `close[T] = (c: Channel[T])`: no more values will be sent: receivers take what is left, then get none (and loops over it end)
- `closed[T] = (c: Channel[T]) -> bool`: it has been closed (values may still wait in it)
- `len[T] = (c: Channel[T]) -> int`: the values waiting in it

#### Waiting

- `next_frame = ()`: wait until the next frame (a game's), letting the others take their turns; outside a game, the same as sleep(0)
- `yield_now = ()`: let the other tasks ready take their turns, then go on: for a task that computes for long without waiting, now and then

## Threads

```gdscript
# example: threads
slow_square = (n: int) -> int:
    total = 0
    loop i in 0..n: total += n
    total

nums = [loop i in 0..16: 1000000 + i]
squares = parallel_map(nums, slow_square)      # spread over every core
print(cpu_count(), "cores", squares[0], squares[15])

halves = parallel_range(8, (i): i / 2.0)  # f(0) .. f(7), in parallel
print(halves)
```

Data parallelism on a pool of worker threads (fork-join).

```gdscript
squares = parallel_map(nums, (x): x * x)
parallel_update(particles, (p): step(p, dt))      # xs[i] = f(xs[i]), in place
rows = parallel_range(height, (y): render_row(y))
```

The function runs on several threads at once: it may read anything, but it must not change globals or the variables it captured (that would be a data race); return results instead. On the web target everything runs on the calling thread.

- `cpu_count = () -> int`: number of hardware threads available to this process
- `parallel_map[T, U] = (xs: T[], f: (T) -> U) -> U[]`: [loop x in xs: f(x)], computed in parallel (f must not change globals or what it captured)
- `parallel_update[T] = (xs: mut T[], f: (T) -> T)`: xs[i] = f(xs[i]) for every element, in parallel
- `parallel_range[U] = (n: int, f: (int) -> U) -> U[]`: [loop i in 0..n: f(i)], computed in parallel

#### Atomics

- `struct Atomic`: A whole number threads change together: parallel code may change it (and no other variable), as a count of work done or a flag that tells the other threads to stop. Copies of an Atomic are the same number.
  ```gdscript
  found = atomic(-1)
  parallel_range(n, (i: int) -> int:
      if found.get() >= 0: return 0          # another thread found it: skip the rest
      if is_answer(i): found.compare_swap(-1, i)
      0
  )
  ```
- `atomic = (v: int = 0) -> Atomic`
- `get = (a: Atomic) -> int`: its value now
- `add = (a: Atomic, v: int) -> int`: add v (negative: subtract) and give the value after
- `set = (a: Atomic, v: int)`: set it to v
- `compare_swap = (a: Atomic, expected: int, v: int) -> bool`: set it to v if it is `expected` (true), or leave it (false): one thread wins
- `set_max = (a: Atomic, v: int)`: make it at least v (the largest of what the threads give)

## Numeric arrays

Elementwise operators (`.+ .* .^`...), `f.(xs)` and n-dimensional arrays are part of the language: see [arrays for numeric work](LANGUAGE.md#8-arrays-for-numeric-work). These are the functions on them.

```gdscript
# example: numeric arrays
xs = linspace(0.0, 1.0, 5)               # [0.0 0.25 0.5 0.75 1.0]
ys = 3.0 .* xs .^ 2.0 .+ 1.0             # element by element, as one loop
print(ys, sqrt.(xs), xs .> 0.5)
print(mean(ys), dot(xs, ys))

m = [1.0 2.0; 3.0 4.0]                   # a 2 x 2 matrix
print(m.shape, m[1, 0], m * [1.0, 1.0], m * transpose(m))
grid = zeros(3, 4)
grid[1, 2] = 5.0
print(grid)
```

Arrays for numeric work, in the spirit of numpy and MATLAB.

T[,] is an n-dimensional array (NDArray[T]): its elements in row-major order, and its shape. How many dimensions it has is part of the value, as in numpy (T[,,] is the same type; the commas are only for the reader). Write a 2-D one as [1 2; 3 4] (rows separated by ';' or line breaks), stack arrays with [a; b], or make one with zeros(2, 3, 4), ones, eye, rand, reshape. m[i, j] (with as many indexes as dimensions) reads and writes an element; m.shape is its shape. Ranges select parts: m[i, ..] is row i, m[.., j] column j, m[1..3, 0..] a block (a..b leaves b out, as everywhere; missing indexes at the end mean whole axes). A part is an array of its own: m[i, ..] is a 1-D T[,], which goes where a T[] is expected (row(m, i) gives the T[] itself, the fast way in a loop). Assigning to one (m[.., 0] = xs, m[0, ..] = 0.0) writes into m. An array of indexes or a mask picks parts along its axis: x[idx, ..] is the rows idx, in that order.

The dotted operators work element by element on arrays of any shape, and broadcast like numpy (a dimension of length 1, or a missing leading one, stretches to fit): a .* b, m .+ 1.0, v .^ 2, xs .< 0.5, and f.(xs) applies f to each element. A whole dotted expression runs as one loop. On 2-D arrays the plain operators follow linear algebra: a * b is the matrix product.

- `type Array[T] = T[]`: another spelling of T[]
- `struct NDArray[T]`
  ```gdscript
  struct NDArray[T]:
      data: T[]
      shape: int[]
  ```

#### Shapes

- `ndarray[T] = (data: T[], shape: int[]) -> T[,]`
- `ndim[T] = (m: T[,]) -> int`
- `size[T] = (m: T[,]) -> int`
- `len[T] = (m: T[,]) -> int`
- `rows[T] = (m: T[,]) -> int`
- `cols[T] = (m: T[,]) -> int`
- `flatten[T] = (m: T[,]) -> T[]`

#### Indexing

- `([])[T] = (m: T[,], i: int) -> T`
- `([])[T] = (m: T[,], i: int, j: int) -> T`
- `([])[T] = (m: T[,], i: int, j: int, k: int) -> T`
- `([])[T] = (m: T[,], i: int, j: int, k: int, l: int) -> T`
- `([]=)[T] = (m: mut T[,], i: int, v: T)`
- `([]=)[T] = (m: mut T[,], i: int, j: int, v: T)`
- `([]=)[T] = (m: mut T[,], i: int, j: int, k: int, v: T)`
- `([]=)[T] = (m: mut T[,], i: int, j: int, k: int, l: int, v: T)`

#### Picking along an axis

- `take[T] = (m: T[,], idx: int[], axis: int = 0) -> T[,]`: the parts of m at these indexes along an axis, in their order (NumPy's take): take(x, idx) is the rows idx of x, as x[idx, ..] and x[idx] are (a mini-batch); with a mask (a bool[] as long as the axis), the parts where it is true
- `take[T] = (m: T[,], mask: bool[], axis: int = 0) -> T[,]`
- `([])[T] = (m: T[,], idx: int[]) -> T[,]`
- `([])[T] = (m: T[,], mask: bool[]) -> T[,]`

#### Joining

- `concat[T] = (a: T[,], b: T[,], axis: int = 0) -> T[,]`: the arrays joined along an axis (NumPy's concatenate): their other dimensions must be the same. concat(a, b) puts b's rows under a's, concat(a, b, 1) b's columns after a's
- `concat[T] = (parts: T[,][], axis: int = 0) -> T[,]`
- `vstack[T] = (parts: T[,][]) -> T[,]`: NumPy's vstack and hstack: rows under rows, a 1-D array being a row (so [a; b] for two), and columns after columns (1-D arrays joined end to end)
- `vstack[T] = (rows: T[][]) -> T[,]`
- `hstack[T] = (parts: T[,][]) -> T[,]`

#### Making arrays

- `zeros = (n: int) -> f64[]`: of another type (NumPy's dtype): `zeros[int](rows, cols)` is an int[,] of zeros
- `zeros = (a: int, b: int) -> f64[,]`
- `zeros = (a: int, b: int, c: int) -> f64[,]`
- `zeros = (a: int, b: int, c: int, d: int) -> f64[,]`
- `zeros[T] = (n: int) -> T[]`
- `zeros[T] = (a: int, b: int) -> T[,]`
- `zeros[T] = (a: int, b: int, c: int) -> T[,]`
- `ones = (n: int) -> f64[]`
- `ones = (a: int, b: int) -> f64[,]`
- `ones = (a: int, b: int, c: int) -> f64[,]`
- `ones = (a: int, b: int, c: int, d: int) -> f64[,]`
- `eye = (n: int) -> f64[,]`: the identity matrix
- `rand = (n: int) -> f64[]`: uniformly random in [0, 1)
- `rand = (a: int, b: int) -> f64[,]`
- `rand = (a: int, b: int, c: int) -> f64[,]`
- `rand = (a: int, b: int, c: int, d: int) -> f64[,]`
- `randn = () -> f64`: normally distributed (mean 0, standard deviation 1)
- `randn = (n: int) -> f64[]`
- `randn = (a: int, b: int) -> f64[,]`
- `randn = (a: int, b: int, c: int) -> f64[,]`
- `randn = (a: int, b: int, c: int, d: int) -> f64[,]`
- `linspace = (a: f64, b: f64, n: int) -> f64[]`: n evenly spaced values from a to b (both included)
- `reshape[T] = (xs: T[], shape: int[]) -> T[,]`
- `reshape[T] = (xs: T[], a: int, b: int) -> T[,]`
- `reshape[T] = (xs: T[], a: int, b: int, c: int) -> T[,]`
- `reshape[T] = (m: T[,], shape: int[]) -> T[,]`
- `reshape[T] = (m: T[,], a: int, b: int) -> T[,]`
- `reshape[T] = (m: T[,], a: int, b: int, c: int) -> T[,]`
- `transpose[T] = (m: T[,]) -> T[,]`: the axes in reverse order (rows and columns swapped, for a 2-D array)
- `row[T] = (m: T[,], i: int) -> T[]`
- `col[T] = (m: T[,], j: int) -> T[]`

#### Reductions

- `sum[T] = (m: T[,]) -> T`
- `min[T] = (m: T[,]) -> T`
- `max[T] = (m: T[,]) -> T`
- `sum = (xs: f64[]) -> f64`: the sum of the elements (floats: added in pairs of halves, so the rounding errors stay small however long the array is: a million 0.1s make 100000.0, as NumPy's sum does)
- `max = (xs: f64[]) -> f64`: (floats: NaN, not a number, wins: one NaN makes the answer NaN, as in NumPy)
- `min = (xs: f64[]) -> f64`
- `sort = (xs: mut f64[], descending: bool = false)`: (floats: NaN after every number, in either order; the numbers in order, equal ones as they were)
- `mean = (xs: f64[]) -> f64`: the average (0 for none)
- `mean = (xs: int[]) -> f64`
- `mean = (m: f64[,]) -> f64`
- `median = (xs: f64[]) -> f64`: the middle value once sorted (the mean of the two middle ones for an even count; 0 for none)
- `median = (xs: int[]) -> f64`
- `std_dev = (xs: f64[]) -> f64`: how far the values are from their mean, on average (the standard deviation of these values: the population's, as numpy's std; Python's statistics.stdev divides by n - 1 instead)
- `std_dev = (xs: int[]) -> f64`
- `sum[T] = (m: T[,], axis: int, keepdims = false) -> T[,]`: along one axis: the array without that axis, each element combining the ones along it (sum(m, 0) adds up the rows of a 2-D array: one sum per column). keepdims = true keeps the axis, with length 1, so that the result broadcasts against m: m .- max(m, 1, keepdims = true) takes each row's largest from the row (NumPy's keepdims)
- `min[T] = (m: T[,], axis: int, keepdims = false) -> T[,]`
- `max[T] = (m: T[,], axis: int, keepdims = false) -> T[,]`
- `mean = (m: f64[,], axis: int, keepdims = false) -> f64[,]`
- `argmax[T] = (m: T[,], axis: int, keepdims = false) -> int[,]`: where the largest (smallest) value along an axis is: its index there, the first of equal ones (argmax(scores, 1): each row's best column, as NumPy's argmax(axis=1))
- `argmin[T] = (m: T[,], axis: int, keepdims = false) -> int[,]`
- `softmax = (m: f64[,], axis: int) -> f64[,]`: e^x of each element over the sum of those along the axis: each row of softmax(scores, 1) is positive and adds up to 1. The largest along the axis is taken away first, so that no e^x overflows (NumPy-style code writes the same, scipy's softmax)
- `softmax = (xs: f64[]) -> f64[]`
- `logsumexp = (m: f64[,], axis: int, keepdims = false) -> f64[,]`: log(sum(exp(m), axis)), without exp overflowing or underflowing: the largest along the axis is taken out first (scipy's logsumexp)
- `logsumexp = (xs: f64[]) -> f64`
- `dot = (a: f64[], b: f64[]) -> f64`: the sum of a[i] * b[i] (kept in four running sums, about twice as fast: rounded a little differently from adding in order, the same for the same input every time)
- `norm = (a: f64[]) -> f64`
- `variance = (xs: f64[]) -> f64`: the variance: the mean of the squared distances from the mean (NumPy's var; std_dev squared)
- `variance = (xs: int[]) -> f64`
- `percentile = (xs: f64[], p: f64) -> f64`: the value p percent of the way through the sorted values (0: the smallest, 50: the median, 100: the largest), between two of them where it falls between (NumPy's percentile)
- `quantile = (xs: f64[], q: f64) -> f64`: the same with q from 0 to 1
- `percentile = (xs: int[], p: f64) -> f64`
- `argmax[T] = (xs: T[]) -> int`: where the largest (smallest) value is: its index, the first of equal ones (-1: empty)
- `argmin[T] = (xs: T[]) -> int`
- `argsort[T] = (xs: T[]) -> int[]`: the indexes of xs in the order that sorts it (equal values keep their order)
- `cumsum = (xs: f64[]) -> f64[]`: running totals: [a, a + b, a + b + c ...]
- `cumsum = (xs: int[]) -> int[]`
- `diff = (xs: f64[]) -> f64[]`: the differences of neighbors: [b - a, c - b ...] (one fewer)
- `diff = (xs: int[]) -> int[]`
- `prod = (xs: f64[]) -> f64`: all of them multiplied
- `prod = (xs: int[]) -> int`
- `histogram = (xs: f64[], bins: int, lo: f64 = 0.0, hi: f64 = 0.0) -> (int[], f64[])`: how many values fall in each of `bins` equal parts of lo..hi (the values' range when lo and hi are equal), and where the parts begin and end (bins + 1 edges; the last part takes hi itself)
- `covariance = (a: f64[], b: f64[]) -> f64`: how a and b vary together: the covariance (divided by n - 1, as NumPy's cov), and the correlation, from -1 to 1 (Pearson's r: NumPy's corrcoef)
- `correlation = (a: f64[], b: f64[]) -> f64`

#### Linear algebra

- `outer = (a: f64[], b: f64[]) -> f64[,]`: the matrix of every a[i] * b[j]
- `diag = (m: f64[,]) -> f64[]`: the diagonal of a square (or any 2-D) matrix, and its sum
- `trace = (m: f64[,]) -> f64`
- `solve = (a: f64[,], b: f64[]) -> f64[]`: x with a * x = b, a square (Gaussian elimination, choosing the largest pivot); a matrix with no inverse panics
- `inverse = (a: f64[,]) -> f64[,]`: the inverse (a * inverse(a) is eye(n)); a matrix with none panics
- `det = (a: f64[,]) -> f64`: the determinant (0 for a matrix with no inverse)

#### Matrix products

- `(*)[T] = (a: T[,], b: T[,]) -> T[,]`: matrix product
- `(*)[T] = (a: T[,], v: T[]) -> T[]`: matrix times column vector
- `(*)[T] = (k: T, m: T[,]) -> T[,]`
- `(*)[T] = (m: T[,], k: T) -> T[,]`
- `(/)[T] = (m: T[,], k: T) -> T[,]`
- `(+)[T] = (a: T[,], b: T[,]) -> T[,]`
- `(-)[T] = (a: T[,], b: T[,]) -> T[,]`

#### As bytes

- `to_bytes[T] = (m: T[,]) -> u8[]`: an n-dimensional array of plain values as bytes: how many dimensions it has and each of them (8 bytes each, little-endian), then its elements' (as to_bytes of its data): read back with ndarray_from_bytes
- `ndarray_from_bytes[T] = (b: u8[]) -> T[,]?`: the array whose bytes are b (from to_bytes): `ndarray_from_bytes[f64](read_bytes(path) ?? [])`; none when b is not one

#### From CSV

- `csv_array = (text: str, sep: u8 = ',') -> f64[,] \| TextError`: the numbers of a CSV text as a 2-D array, a row of the array for each row of the text (NumPy's loadtxt(path, delimiter=",")): `x = csv_array(read_file("data.csv") ?? "")`. A first row that is not all numbers (a header) is left out; an empty field, or NA, is NaN. A TextError says where a field is not a number, or a row has another number of fields than the first

## The window, the game loop and input

A program that defines `update(dt: f64)` or `draw()` opens a window and calls them every frame. More on windows, screen sizes and the game loop: [the language](LANGUAGE.md#12-programs-and-the-game-loop).

```gdscript
# example: the window, the game loop and input
window("Input", 960, 540)               # the title, and the size of the 2D area

pos = vec2(480, 270)
trail: vec2[] = []

update = (dt: f64):                      # every frame; dt: the seconds since the last one
    global pos
    pos += input_axis() * 320 * dt  # arrows, W A S D, or a gamepad's stick
    if mouse_down(.left): pos = mouse_pos()
    if key_pressed(.space): trail.clear()
    if key_pressed(.f): toggle_fullscreen()
    if key_pressed(.tab): show_stats(not stats_shown())     # frame times, and the zones' below
    zone("trail")                         # a part of the frame, timed in the statistics
    trail.push(pos)
    if trail.len() > 60: trail.remove_at(0)
    zone_end()

draw = ():                               # every frame, after update
    clear(rgb(0.08, 0.09, 0.12))
    loop i in 0.. and p in trail: circle(p, 4, rgba(0.3, 0.7, 0.9, i / 60.0))
    circle(pos, 18, ORANGE)
    text("move: arrows, W A S D or click   space: clear   F: fullscreen   Tab: statistics", vec2(20, 20), 20)
    text("{fps():.0} fps, {elapsed():.1} s", vec2(20, 500), 18, GRAY)
```

### window.jo

Windows, the frame loop and input. Linux: Wayland (or X11), drawn with Vulkan (or OpenGL ES 3, or the software renderer: SLOPPY_SOFTWARE=1); Windows: Win32 and Vulkan or OpenGL; macOS: Cocoa and OpenGL. The system's libraries are loaded at runtime. Web: a <canvas> with WebGL2.

- `enum Key:`
  ```gdscript
  enum Key:
      unknown, a, b, c, d, e, f, g, h, i, j, k, l, m, n, o, p, q, r, s, t, u, v, w, x, y, z,
      n0, n1, n2, n3, n4, n5, n6, n7, n8, n9,
      space, enter, escape, tab, backspace, delete, insert, home, end, page_up, page_down,
      left, right, up, down, shift, ctrl, alt, super,
      f1, f2, f3, f4, f5, f6, f7, f8, f9, f10, f11, f12,
      minus, equals, comma, period, slash, semicolon, quote, lbracket, rbracket, backslash, grave,
      kp0, kp1, kp2, kp3, kp4, kp5, kp6, kp7, kp8, kp9, kp_period, kp_plus, kp_minus, kp_star, kp_slash
  ```
- `enum Mouse: left, middle, right`
- `key_down = (k: Key) -> bool`: true while the key is held
- `key_pressed = (k: Key) -> bool`: true only in the frame the key was pressed
- `key_released = (k: Key) -> bool`
- `key_typed = (k: Key) -> bool`: true in the frame the key was pressed, and again each time it repeats while held down (for moving through text or menus); characters typed arrive through text_input()
- `shortcut = (k: Key, shift: bool = false) -> bool`: true in the frame Ctrl and k are pressed together, and as it repeats (on a Mac and in a browser Cmd too), for a tool's keys: `if shortcut(.s): save()`; shift: with Shift held too, as Ctrl+Shift+Z (without it, Shift must not be held). Browsers keep some for themselves (Ctrl+N, Ctrl+W, Ctrl+T) and the page never sees them.
- `mouse_pos = () -> vec2`: (in the 2D units of the screen: see screen_size and screen.jo)
- `mouse_delta = () -> vec2`
- `mouse_wheel = () -> f64`
- `mouse_down = (b: Mouse) -> bool`
- `mouse_pressed = (b: Mouse) -> bool`
- `mouse_released = (b: Mouse) -> bool`
- `text_input = () -> str`: characters typed this frame
- `pasted_text = () -> str`: the text pasted this frame (ctrl+V, cmd+V on a Mac, a web page's paste, a text field's Paste button on a phone): the clipboard's text then; "" in other frames. For a widget of a game's own (the hud_ text fields take it themselves)

#### A fixed time step

- `fixed_update = (dt: f64, step: (f64) -> void, rate: f64 = 60.0) -> f64`: step(dt) at a fixed rate, `rate` times a second whatever the frame rate: as many times as the time since the last frame holds (none in some frames of a 144 Hz screen, two in some at 30), the rest kept for the next frame. Physics and jumps come out the same at any frame rate (Godot's _physics_process, Unity's FixedUpdate): In step, a press (key_pressed, mouse_pressed, gamepad_pressed, action_pressed, and their released) is seen in one step only, the first after it, even one that came in a frame with no step. It gives back how far the time is into the next step (0 to 1): to draw what moves between where the last two steps put it, `mix(prev_pos, pos, f32(k))`.
  ```gdscript
  update = (dt: f64): fixed_update(dt, physics)
  physics = (dt: f64):                 # dt: 1 / 60, always
      if action_pressed("jump"): vel.y = -JUMP
      ...
  ```
- `input_axis = () -> vec2`: arrows/WASD (or a gamepad's left stick and d-pad) as a direction vector (y down)
- `screen_width = () -> int`: The screen's size in 2D drawing units: the size given to window() (the design size), whatever the window's real size; screen.jo says how it maps to the window (screen_fit). pixel_size() is the window's real size.
- `screen_height = () -> int`
- `screen_size = () -> vec2`
- `pixel_size = () -> vec2`
- `aspect_ratio = () -> f64`: width / height of the picture 3D draws into (for a projection: perspective(fov, aspect_ratio(), ...)): the window's, or the design area's when the screen fit shows just that (screen.jo)
- `visible_rect = () -> Rect`: the part of the 2D plane the screen shows, as a Rect (x, y, w, h): with screen_fit(.expand) (the default) the design area centered in a window of any shape and what is around it (draw backgrounds over this to fill the window); with bars, just the design area
- `frame_time = () -> f64`
- `time_scale = (s: f64)`: how fast the game's time runs: update gets dt times this (0.5: slow motion, 0: paused, while drawing, input and sound go on); frame_time() is the scaled dt, fps() and elapsed() are not
- `time_scale = () -> f64`: the scale set with time_scale (1 to start with)
- `fps = () -> f64`
- `elapsed = () -> f64`
- `frame_number = () -> int`
- `quit = ()`: end the game (the window closes once this frame is done). A web page cannot close itself: there it does nothing (can_quit() is false, so a menu can leave its Quit button out)
- `can_quit = () -> bool`

#### Frames drawn on demand: tools, editors, forms

- `redraw_on_input = (on: bool = true)`: A game draws 60 frames a second; a tool that waits for its user need not. With redraw_on_input(), a frame is drawn only when something may have changed: a key, the mouse moving, a click or the wheel, text typed, the window resized or focused, files dropped, a file dialog's answer (and two frames after each, for what a frame's drawing sets off), at the time redraw_after asked for, or after request_redraw(). In between the program sleeps (sound still plays). update's dt is the time since the last frame (0.1 s at most). Something that moves on its own (an animation, a timer shown, a tween) calls request_redraw() in each frame it is not done in (tweens, hud_scroll's gliding and a text field's blinking caret do so themselves). While the program fetches from the network or has tasks, a frame comes at least 10 times a second, so that their answers are seen. Screenshot runs draw every frame as usual. On the web too: the page asks for no animation frames while it waits.
  ```gdscript
  redraw_on_input()
  draw = (): ...                      # a frame only as something happens
  ```
- `request_redraw = ()`: draw another frame soon, even with nothing typed or moved (redraw_on_input): while an animation runs, once in each frame
- `redraw_after = (seconds: f64)`: draw a frame `seconds` from now at the latest (redraw_on_input): a clock that shows the time, a message that goes away, a caret that blinks
- `set_fullscreen = (on: bool = true)`
- `toggle_fullscreen = ()`
- `is_fullscreen = () -> bool`
- `can_fullscreen = () -> bool`: whether set_fullscreen can fill the screen: false in an iPhone's Safari, which has no fullscreen for a page's canvas (a game can leave its Fullscreen button out there)
- `clipboard = () -> str`: the text on the system clipboard ("" if none, or not text). In a web page the browser lets a page read it only as it is pasted: this is the text last pasted (ctrl+V, or a text field's Paste button on a phone) into the page.
- `set_clipboard = (s: str)`
- `can_share = () -> bool`: the system's menu for sending text to another app (a chat, Messages, mail), where there is one: a web page on a phone, and in some desktop browsers. In a page the menu opens as the tap or click that asked ends (browsers allow it only then)
  ```gdscript
  if can_share() and hud_button(r, "Share"): share(invite_link)
  ```
- `share = (text: str)`
- `sideways_hint = (on: bool)`: a phone held upright, playing a game made for a screen wider than tall: a note over the game to turn the phone sideways (on to start with; the game goes on under it). Off for a game laid out for a phone either way up (visible_rect says what shows).
- `upright_hint = (on: bool)`: the other way: a phone held sideways, playing a game made for a screen taller than wide (as window("...", 360, 640)): a note to turn the phone upright (on to start with); off for a game laid out for both
- `mouse_lock = (on: bool = true) -> bool`: lock the mouse pointer to the window and hide it, for mouse look: the pointer stays put and its motion keeps arriving through mouse_delta(). false where the platform cannot. (A web page gets the lock at the next click into it, as browsers require; Escape releases it there.)
- `is_mouse_locked = () -> bool`: (in a screenshot run, what the game asked for: the desktop's focus coming and going would make the run differ from one time to the next)
- `window_focused = () -> bool`: the window (or the web page) has the keyboard's focus: false while the player is in another window or tab, as after a click outside an itch.io frame (a game may pause then)
- `touches = () -> vec2[]`: the fingers on a touch screen (a phone's, in a web page): where each is, in the screen's units as mouse_pos, in the order they landed. The first finger also is the mouse, its left button held while it is down, so a game made for the mouse works with one finger; more fingers (on screen buttons: left, right, jump) are only here. Empty without a touch screen.
- `touch_ids = () -> int[]`: each finger's number, in the order of touches(): the same for as long as that finger stays down, and another for the next one to land, so a game can follow each finger from frame to frame (what it landed on, how far it moved since the last frame: a drag that turns a camera)
  ```gdscript
  loop k in 0.. and id in touch_ids(): at = touches()[k]
  ```
- `touch_input = () -> bool`: whether the pointer is a finger: the last pointer input was a touch, not the mouse (a finger lifted leaves mouse_pos where it was: hover highlights mean nothing then)
- `touch_device = () -> bool`: whether the screen is a touch screen, its main pointer a finger (a phone or a tablet: a page asks the browser), known before the first touch: on-screen buttons from the start, say. touch_input() says whether the last input was a finger

#### A press of the left button (or of the first finger): taps, drags, swipes

- `claim_press = ()`: the press now held (of the left button, or a finger) is the game's own, for a drag of its own: a hud_scroll list it went down in does not move with it, and letting go of it clicks no button. Call it in the frame it goes down (on mouse_pressed), as the drag starts:
  ```gdscript
  if mouse_pressed(.left) and contains(row, mouse_pos()):
      dragging = i
      claim_press()
  ```
- `press_claimed = () -> bool`: whether the press now held was claimed (claim_press), or moved a hud_scroll list
- `swipe = () -> vec2?`: a drag of a finger (or the mouse) that just ended, 30 points long or more: which way it went most, vec2(-1, 0) left, vec2(1, 0) right, vec2(0, -1) up or vec2(0, 1) down, in the frame it lets go; none otherwise, and for a drag begun on a hud_ box or one a scrolling list moved with
  ```gdscript
  if let d = swipe(): slide(int(d.x), int(d.y))
  ```
- `long_press = (seconds: f64 = 0.5) -> bool`: true once, in the frame a finger (or the left mouse button) has been held down `seconds` without moving: what it is on, said more about, say. Letting go after it is no tap of a button

#### Points, a phone's notch, the design size: one layout for screens of any size

- `pixel_ratio = () -> f64`: how many of the window's pixels make a point (a page's CSS pixel: about 1/160 inch on a phone, 1/96 on a desktop screen): the page's devicePixelRatio as it is drawn, or the desktop's scale (Wayland's, a Retina Mac's, Windows'), 1 without one
- `css_pixel = () -> f64`: a point's size in 2D units: what must be the same size to a finger or an eye on any screen, a phone's or a desktop's, is sized in these (a button 48 * css_pixel() tall fits a finger; text 16 * css_pixel() high reads as a page's)
- `safe_rect = () -> Rect`: the part of visible_rect() clear of a phone's notch, rounded corners and home bar (in a page on such a phone; all of visible_rect() elsewhere): what must be seen or tapped goes inside it, backgrounds over all of visible_rect()
- `design_size = (w: int, h: int)`: change the design size (the 2D units, as given to window()) while the game runs: one layout for a phone held upright, another for a screen wider than tall, say (the window keeps its size; screen_fit says how the new units fill it), at once: in update, before drawing (with a letterbox fit or a fixed resolution, from the next frame)
  ```gdscript
  p = pixel_size() / f32(pixel_ratio())           # the screen in points
  if p.y > p.x: design_size(360, 640) else: design_size(1280, 720)
  ```
- `show_cursor = (on: bool)`: show or hide the mouse pointer over the window (hidden: a game drawing its own crosshair or cursor); unlike mouse_lock, it still moves and mouse_pos() follows it
- `set_cursor = (c: Cursor)`: the mouse pointer's shape over the window, from the end of this frame on: .arrow (the usual), .ibeam over text to type in, .hand over a link, .crosshair, .move over what drags, .resize_ew, .resize_ns, .resize_nwse and .resize_nesw over an edge or a corner that resizes, .not_allowed, .wait while busy; .hidden is show_cursor(false), and any other shape shows it again. While it is .arrow, a hud_text_field or ui_text_field under the pointer shows an I-beam. (In a web page, the browser's cursors; on Wayland, where the compositor has cursor shapes)
  ```gdscript
  over = divider.contains(mouse_pos())
  set_cursor(if over or dragging: Cursor.resize_ew else: Cursor.arrow)
  ```
- `enum Cursor: arrow, ibeam, hand, crosshair, move, resize_ew, resize_ns, resize_nwse, resize_nesw, not_allowed, wait, hidden`
- `window = (title: str = "", width: int = 1280, height: int = 720)`: Open the game window (called automatically when the program defines update/draw).
- `set_title = (title: str)`: change the window's title while the game runs (a level's name, the score); in a web page, the page's title

### files.jo

A user's files: dropped onto the window, or chosen in the system's file dialog. The same calls everywhere; what a path is differs. Natively a path is where the file is on the disk. In a web page the browser gives the page a copy of the file, not where it is: the page keeps it in its own files, which read_file, read_bytes and load_image read, under the file's name.

```gdscript
loop path in dropped_files(): open_doc(read_file(path) ?? "")

if key_pressed(.o) and key_down(.ctrl): asked = open_file_dialog([".txt", ".md"])
if asked >= 0:
    if let paths = file_dialog_result(asked):        # (each frame: none while it is open)
        asked = -1
        if paths.len() > 0: open_doc(read_file(paths[0]) ?? "")      # ([]: cancelled)
```

Dialogs: on Windows the system's (the program waits while it is open); on Linux zenity's or kdialog's, when one of them is installed (else the answer is at once: no files); on macOS AppleScript's (osascript). In a web page, the browser's file picker: it opens only as a click, a tap or a key ends (browsers allow it only then; asked for at another time, it opens at the next one). Drops: Windows, X11, Wayland and web pages (not yet macOS).

Testing aids: SLOPPY_FILE_DIALOG="a.txt|b.txt" answers every dialog with those paths, at once ("": cancelled); SLOPPY_INPUT's 5:drop=a.txt drops a file in frame 5.

- `dropped_files = () -> str[]`: the files dropped onto the window since the last frame: their paths (in a web page, the names of the page's copies, which read_file reads), in this frame only; [] in most frames
- `open_file_dialog = (types: str[] = [], multiple: bool = false, title: str = "") -> int`: ask the user for a file to open, in the system's dialog: an id for file_dialog_result, which gives the answer once the user has chosen. types: the extensions to show (".png", "jpg"), [] for any file; multiple: more than one may be chosen; title: the dialog's own
- `save_file_dialog = (name: str = "", types: str[] = [], title: str = "") -> int`: ask the user where to save a file: as open_file_dialog, with a name to start with. The answer is the path to write to. In a web page the answer is `name` at once, a file of the page's own: write it there, then download_file(path) hands it to the browser, which saves it where the user says (natively download_file does nothing more; the same code works everywhere)
  ```gdscript
  if let p = file_dialog_result(asked):
      if p.len() > 0:
          write_file(p[0], text)
          download_file(p[0])
  ```
- `file_dialog_result = (d: int) -> str[]?`: a file dialog's answer (open_file_dialog's or save_file_dialog's id), once the user has chosen: the paths chosen, [] if the dialog was cancelled; none while it is open. Given once: asking again stops the program (a mistake: keep the answer when it comes)

### stats.jo

A frame statistics overlay: frame times as a graph, CPU time, time handing frames to the screen, frames that took much longer than usual (stutter), and the time each zone of the frame takes (zone("physics") ... zone_end(): update and draw are zones already, as are the 3D scene, its shadows and post-processing), on the CPU and, through Vulkan, on the GPU. show_stats() turns it on, as do SLOPPY_STATS=1 and, in a web page, ?stats in the URL. SLOPPY_STATS=log (?stats=log) prints the same numbers every two seconds instead, and each long frame as it happens.

- `show_stats = (on: bool = true)`
- `stats_shown = () -> bool`

#### Zones: named parts of a frame, timed
